No. After the temptations, Luke follows Jesus back to Galilee and ultimately to Nazareth, his hometown, where we find him in âthe synagogue on the sabbath dayâ (4:16). Friends and family members and acquaintances, hearing of his earlier activities in Capernaum (see 4:23), wait with bated breath as he stands to read from scripture: âThe Spirit of the Lord is upon me,â he recites, âbecause he hath anointed me to preach the gospel to the poor; he hath sent me to heal the brokenheartedâ (4:18). In these words stands the declaration of his authorityâin the heart of a synagogue service (see the Note on 4:18; the Analysis on 4:16â30). But there is more.

The next Sabbath we find Jesus down in Capernaum, more than twenty miles away, inside the synagogue of course (see 4:31, 33). Remarkably, inside the building, âthere was a man, which had a spirit of an unclean devilâ (4:33; see the Note thereon). To bring relief to the man, Jesus rebukes the unclean demon, driving him out of the man (see 4:34â35). We can predict the reaction of the congregation members: âthey were all amazed.â Then Luke quotes the response of some in attendance: âwith authority and power he commandeth the unclean spirits, and they come outâ (4:36; emphasis added). Here, on the lips of attendees is the affirmation of Jesusâ authority and power after they witness a manifestation of his authority and power inside the synagogue. We emphasize, as Luke does, that both Jesusâ miracle and the congregationâs immediate response take place in a synagogue service, on the Sabbath day. What can we therefore conclude? It becomes clear that Jesus not only reverences the synagogue and the Sabbath day but also consciously chooses the synagogue in Nazareth to announce his authority and the synagogue in Capernaum to show his power. The synagogueâa place of worship, of prayer, of scripture reading, of devotion, of holy ordinances, of sacred learningâis the place where Jesus responds to the devilâs challenge about power and authority (see the Note on 4:36). Because within days he will call the first four men who will serve as Apostles (see 5:1â11), it is evident that the synagogue, an institution of worship, forms the launchpad for his own church organization (see the Analysis on 4:1â13).

Within Lukeâs Gospel we begin to see much of the outline of his church organization. Naturally, Jesus first calls the Twelve. And he guides the process. He first calls key men to follow him, initially the two pairs of fishermen brothers, Peter and Andrew, James and John, and then Levi, the customs agent (see 5:1â11, 27â28; the Notes on 5:6â7). After reaching out to these brethren, he allows the natural process of personal attraction to him and his message to swell the ranks of followers so that, a few weeks later, he can go âinto a mountain to prayâ about choosing âtwelveâ men from their number âwhom also he named apostlesâ (6:12â13). That same morning, after calling these twelve men, he leads them down to âthe plainâ where they meet the larger number âof his disciplesâ as well as âa great multitude of people,â some of whom come from far away âto hear him, and to be healed of their diseasesâ (6:17). The scene becomes the formal announcement of the Twelve, the anchors of the churchâs organization (see the Notes on 6:17; 9:1â2; the Analysis on 5:1â11; 6:17â19; 9:1â6).106 But Jesus does not stop here. He regularly offers instruction to disciples, more specifically to the Twelve, throughout his ministry (see 12:36â38; 16:8; 22:25â27; the Notes on 8:51, 54; 9:50, 60; 10:2; 12:39, 41â43, 48; the Analysis on 9:49â50; 12:41â48).

We turn next to women. Conceptually, Jesus features them as full members of his kingdom in a simple pair of parables. In the first, he compares the kingdom to âa grain of mustard seed, which a man took, and cast into his garden,â underscoring work typically done by a man. He then compares the kingdom to âleaven, which a woman took and hid in three measures of meal,â highlighting an act usually performed by a woman (13:18â21). In these two brief illustrations, Jesus turns his social and legal world upside-down. For in the Ancient Near East, in any period, women stand as second-class citizens. No so for Jesusâ kingdom. They will enjoy full membership on an equal plane with men (see the Note on 13:21; the Analysis on 13:18â21).

On a practical level, following Lukeâs first notice of âthe twelveâ accompanying Jesus on a preaching tour, he writes about âcertain womenâ from Galilee who receive ministrations from Jesus and also minister âunto him of their substanceâ (8:1â3). Such a place of honor, next to the Twelve, signals their high importance among Jesusâ closest followers. They will reappear at the cross and at the tomb, following him to Jerusalem and thereby becoming first-rank witnesses of his resurrection (see 23:49, 55â56; 24:1â11, 22â24).107 In addition, though not noted, they most certainly assist in the preparations for and the serving of the Last Supper (see the Note on 22:8). Their involvement in these significant events, and their close tie to the Twelve, underscore their roles as primary participants in Jesusâ emerging church (see Acts 1:14).108 From what we can learn about them, they appear very much like an ancient Relief Society (see the Notes on 8:1â3; 23:56; the Analysis on 22:7â13; 23:50â56).

Another highly visible group consists of the Seventy (see 10:1â12, 17â20). Jesus calls these men from the growing entourage of followers and sends them on a preaching mission. To them he gives instructions similar to the Twelve (see 9:1â6); to them he gives priesthood power to âheal the sickâ (10:9); for them he suspends food laws, indicating that their mission will reach out to Gentiles (see the Notes on 10:7â8). Thus Jesus creates a second quorum of leaders in his church, this one with a charge to reach beyond fellow Jews to embrace Gentiles (see the Notes on 8:38â39; 10:1; the Analysis on 8:26â40; 10:1â12).109 Modern scripture affirms this very assessment: âThe Seventy are also called to preach the gospel, and to be especial witnesses unto the Gentiles and in all the worldâthus differing from other officers in the church in the duties of their callingâ (D&C 107:25).

Little noticed are individuals who function essentially as heralds for Jesus, much as John does, although John prepares for Jesus generally and these people form an advance team that travels to specific destinations.110 As we have already seen, when Jesus turns deliberately toward Jerusalem and away from Galilee, he âsent messengers before his face.â Their task is not to preach but âto make ready for himâ (9:52). They are to announce Jesusâ coming in towns and villages before he arrives, thus assuring that citizens have opportunity to see and hear the Son of God and alerting townspeople that a large group will be arriving so that they have goods at hand to supply them with food and places to lodge. These messengers are an obvious key to the success of Jesusâ preaching mission. In the modern church, such people are Aaronic Priesthood holders, who are to assist church leaders âto make appointments, and to prepare the way . . . this is the way that mine apostles, in ancient days, built up my churchâ (D&C 84:107â8), duplicating the pattern established here by Jesus (see the Notes on 9:49â50, 52; the Analysis on 9:49â50).

In light of what we introduce above, we conclude that long before Jesus arrives in Jerusalem for the last time, he organizes his church and establishes important patterns of missionary work and church service. He authorizes men and women and perhaps even youths. His intent is to spread his message to as many people as possible and to give them opportunity to accept that message.

C. Keys

Only Matthew documents a known statement from the mortal Jesus about keys: âI will give unto thee [Peter] the keys of the kingdom of heaven,â he intones, âand whatsoever thou shalt bind on earth shall be bound in heaven: and whatsoever thou shalt loose on earth shall be loosed in heavenâ (Matt. 16:19). Plainly, these keys open the powers of sealing and unsealing earthly matters, rendering them effective or ineffective in the next life. In Luke, keys lie just out of sight, but are present nonetheless. He quotes Jesusâ summarizing of a famine condition in the days of Elijah: âmany widows were in Israel in the days of Elias, when the heaven was shut up three years and six monthsâ (4:25). Clearly implicit are keys that lock and unlock the heavens, blocking or allowing passage of blessings from one sphere to another (see Rev. 11:6).111

Near the end of his Gospel, Luke writes of the experience of the two disciples who walk with and then refresh themselves with the Resurrected Christ in Emmaus. At a certain moment, âtheir eyes were opened, and they knew [Jesus]â (24:31). For a second time, keys are assumed, this time to open the eyes. In the very next verse, the two disciples say âone to another, Did not our heart burn within us, . . . while he opened to us the scriptures?â (24:32). As is now obvious, keys are required to open our understanding of the scriptures. In fact, this same use of keys plays out later that evening in the presence of a number of disciples: âThen opened he their understanding, that they might understand the scripturesâ (24:45). These keys, designed to lock and unlock the heavens and to lock and unlock peopleâs eyes and the scriptures, differ in function from those promised to Peter. But they are just as real and point to the variety of keys that the Savior turns in our behalf (see 11:52; 13:25; Rev. 1:18; 3:7; 9:1; 11:6; 20:1; the Notes on 24:31â32, 45).112

Lukeâs narrative thus presents a series of passages that have to do with keys, specifically from the mortal viewpoint, that is, how they affect us, whether opening our eyes or opening our ability to comprehend. Modern scripture offers a similar picture, but from the divine perspective: âthe light [of Christ] which shineth, which giveth you light . . . is the same light that quickeneth your understandingsâ (D&C 88:11; see the Notes on 24:31â32). The Savior possesses the keys to open the door to proper understanding, namely, to understand as God does.113

D. Delay of Second Coming

Almost all commentators agree that statements about the delay of the Saviorâs Second Coming arise from early Christians themselves who, expecting him to return immediately, have to deal with his nonappearance. But indicators exist that Jesus is the author of this delay, an observation that rests firmly in other scripture. The earliest signal appears in a vision of Enoch wherein, after seeing the resurrection, the seer asks the Lord, âWilt thou not come again upon the earth?â What is the response? âAs I live,â declares the Lord in an oath, âeven so will I come in the last days, in the days of wickedness and vengeanceâ (Moses 7:59â60). Thus, long before Jesus comes upon the earth as an infant, he promises that he will return again, but only âin the last days.â In this connection, those who are converted in the age of the Apostles come to Christ because he will âmanifest [himself] unto them . . . by the Holy Ghost,â not by personal appearance (3 Ne. 15:23). The Apostle Paul spells out the delay in different terms. To the Thessalonian saints he pleads that they not be âshaken in mind, or be troubled, neither by spirit, nor by word, nor by letter as [if] from us, as that the day of Christ is at hand.â That day will only arrive after âthere come a falling away first,â that is, an apostasy (2 Thes. 2:2â3).

In Lukeâs record, we encounter Jesusâ first attempt to give contour to this teaching when, at a distance from Jerusalem, he laments, âO Jerusalem, Jerusalem, which killest the prophets, . . . how often would I have gathered thy children together, . . . and ye would not! . . . Verily I say unto you, Ye shall not see me, until the time come when ye shall say, Blessed is he that cometh in the name of the Lordâ (13:34â35). The Joseph Smith Translation adds a strip of authenticity to this lament by noting Jesusâ action in an insertion before this saying: âin this very hour he began to weep over Jerusalem.â The JST then buttresses the saying by adjusting and adding words of Jesus in its midst: âverily I say unto you, Ye shall not know me, until ye have received from the hand of the Lord a just recompense for all your sinsâ (JST 13:34, 36; emphasis added; see the Notes on 13:34â35; the Analysis on 13:31â35). The sense is that, after the inhabitants suffer, the Savior mercifully will allow them to come to know him as he is (see Isa. 40:1â2).

No more than a few weeks later, Jesus gives voice again to his Second Coming. On this occasion, he addresses specifically his disciples: âThe days will come, when ye shall desire to see one of the days of the Son of man, and ye shall not see it.â Why not? Because the Son of Manâs coming will be later. He follows these words almost immediately with a further hint at delay: âas the lightning, . . . so shall also the Son of man be in his day,â coming suddenly and brilliantly (17:22, 24). The Joseph Smith Translation adds weight to the distant approach of the Second Coming by changing this last verse to read: âas the light of the morning, . . . so shall also the Son of man be in his dayâ (JST 17:24; emphasis added). The brilliance remains, but the suddenness diminishes (see the Notes on 17:22â24; the Analysis on 17:20â37). In sum, it is just as reasonable to see Jesus offering such teaching to his disciples as they approach the capital city as it is to try to make this instruction a part of the drama-filled days in Jerusalem.114 In light of the JST additions and adjustments, it is clear that Jesus announces the delay, not others.

As evidence that Jesus and his followers discuss his Second Coming before arriving in the city we cite his words repeated to them as they gather together atop the Mount of Olives for his important sermon there: âAs ye have asked of me concerning the signs of my [second] coming, in the day when I shall come in my glory in the clouds of heavenâ (D&C 45:16). Here is the tipoff, in Jesusâ own words, that on earlier occasions he and they converse about his Second Coming. But what about the delay? Jesus goes on: âconcerning Jerusalem . . . when that day shall come, shall a remnant [of Jerusalemâs residents] be scattered among all nations; But they shall be gathered again; but they shall remain until the times of the Gentiles be fulfilledâ (D&C 45:24â25). Clearly, Jesus envisions a long delay that involves a scattering and gathering of his people as well as blessings coming to the Gentiles. Concerning that era of the Gentiles, âhe answered them, and said, In the generation in which the times of the Gentiles shall be fulfilled, there shall be signsâ (JST 21:25). Notably, the signs that Jesus discloses in his sermon on the Mount of Olives will appear chiefly in that distant age (see the Notes on 21:25â28; the Analysis on 21:25â28).

With the help of modern scripture, therefore, we arrive at a clear understanding that Jesus himself teaches about a delay of his Second Coming. To be sure, some early Christians mistakenly believe that his reappearance is near. But that expectation of an immediate return is incorrect, as Paul reminds church members living in Thessalonica (see 2 Thes. 2:2â3).

E. Satan

Satan is a real personality, not a figment of peopleâs imagination. And, as Luke shows in his narrative, Satan possesses genuine powers, including his own kingdom (see the Note on 11:18). For example, he has the ability to diminish, even take, divine light that a person receives through Godâs word; he can degrade a personâs well-being as he does to the woman afflicted for eighteen years; he possesses power to break a personâs commitment to the Savior as he seeks to do with Peter; more frightening still, he can directly influence a person to do his will, if the person becomes a willing host, as Judas does. Notably, modern scripture affirms this brief photograph of the devil (see the Notes on 8:12; 13:16; 22:3, 31; the Analysis on 8:26â40; also Alma 5:41; 34:35; 40:13; 3 Ne. 18:18; D&C 10:10, 15, 20â21; 29:40; 78:10). But there is more.

Satanâs minions come in multiples and can behave in the same way that he does. After all, he captures a third of the spirits in the premortal struggle for dominance and they are now under his influence (see Moses 4:6; D&C 29:36â37; Rev. 12:4). For instance, seven come to inhabit Mary Magdalene and, in a merciful moment, are cast out; a large number, that call themselves âLegion,â take possession of the gentile man on the east side of the Sea of Galilee and, as in Maryâs situation, are driven out by Jesusâ power (see the Notes on 8:2, 27, 29â33). Hence, the devil possesses an army of willing demons that do his bidding (see the Notes on 11:17â18).

But Satan and his demon forces are subject to the Saviorâs power. Again and again in Gospel stories, Jesus forces them from their temporary roosts in accord with a prophecy uttered more than a century before his birth: â[the Savior] shall cast out devils [in his mortal ministry], or the evil spirits which dwell in the hearts of the children of menâ (Mosiah 3:6). Jesus first signals that he can both resist Satanâs temptations and also overcome them when the two of them tussle in the desert (see 4:1â13). Within days, Jesus follows his victory in the wilderness by summarily tossing out one of Satanâs minions from a beleaguered man in the Capernaum synagogue (see 4:33â35). Plainly, Jesus is taking back territory over which Satan exerts power (see the Analysis on 10:17â20). Further, as we have noticed, Jesus asserts his own power over the devil when he portrays âa strong manâ in âhis palaceâ whose âgoods are in peaceâ until assailed by âa stronger than heâ who overcomes âhim, [and] . . . taketh away from him all his armour wherein he trustedâ (11:21â22). This scene pictures Satan, the âstrong man,â resting at ease in someoneâs home, now designated âhis palace,â until âa stronger than he,â the Savior, comes along and pushes him out of that home. The contest is over control of the home wherein Jesus challenges Satanâs grip. It is significant that Jesus invokes images of the home to affirm his power over the devil and his minions (see the Notes on 11:21â22; the Analysis on 11:14â28).

V. Character of Lukeâs Gospel

A. Date and Place of Composition

The question of dating Lukeâs Gospel always ties to the Jewish revolt of AD 66â70. Do hints exist that he is writing after the war? Yes and no. On the yes side, stand passages that stress the law-abiding character of Christians, often in contrast to their Jewish neighbors (see 1:6; 2:4â5, 22, 24, 27, 39, 42, 51; 20:46â47; etc.; Acts 13:50; 14:2, 19; 17:5, 13; etc.). This dimension is evidently a natural outcome of the war wherein most inhabitants of the Roman world do not know how to distinguish Christians and Jews and may well assume that all Christians are sympathizers and fellow citizens with the Jewish rebels. Luke apparently senses the need to make a distinction for his reading public (see the Analysis on 2:21â24). Luke constructs the second leg of the yes response when he writes that, before him, âmany have taken in hand to set forth . . . a declarationâ of Jesusâ story (1:1). If Markâs Gospel is among those many, and it likely is, then Luke writes his Gospel following the war, judging that Mark composes his Gospel in the late sixties of the first century (see section III.G above).115 The third part of the yes answer arises from Jesusâ predictions of the terrible fate of the city during the war, predictions seemingly too detailed to look forward to Jerusalemâs fall but, rather, grow out of hindsight (see 13:35; 19:41â44; 21:6, 20â24; 23:28â31).116 But this latter point rests squarely on the view that no one can predict exact details in the future, a notion that modern studies and modern scripture undercut (see D&C 45:18â25).117 To be sure, Luke likely composes his Gospel after the fall of the city, but he is a faithful reporter of the Saviorâs predictions (see the Analysis on 19:41â44; 21:5â6, 20â24).

On the no side, we find no clear cases of Lukeâs account looking back to the fall of Jerusalem. First, the usual attempt to seize on Jesusâ language of 21:20 (âye shall see Jerusalem compassed with armiesâ) falls flat because we know that Jesus can predict the future, even with high precision. Second, this event becomes a major moment in the ancient world and we find no allusions to the cityâs collapse in Lukeâs book of Acts where he might foreshadow the punishment of Jewish officials and individuals through the cityâs destruction, punishment for unleashing persecution against the Saviorâs followers (see Acts 4:1â3; 5:17â18, 26â28; 6:9â13; 7:54â59; 8:1â3; etc.). Third, Luke shows no knowledge of the Apostle Paulâs fate in his book of Acts, how his hearing before the emperor goes (see Acts 25:9â12; 28:16), whether Paul travels to Spain as he plans (see Rom. 15:24, 28), or whether he is executed following Neroâs burning of Rome in AD 64, as hinted by Clement of Rome, who writes about AD 96 (see 1 Clem. 5:3â7).118

In this light, it is impossible to fix a date, even an approximate date, for Luke writing his two-volume work, the Gospel and the book of Acts. On balance, it seems that a date between AD 70 and 80 settles most easily within the evidence, all of which is circumstantial.

The place where Luke composes his Gospel remains unknown. Early and modern attempts locate Lukeâs efforts in Rome, Achaia, Boeotia, Antioch, or Caesarea; all rely on little or no evidence.119 Most authors will agree that Luke writes his Gospel outside of ancient Palestine. But in light of his grasp of geography, even this judgment is subject to question (see section V.C below).

B. Luke the Man

Luke, âa messenger of Jesus Christâ (JST 1:1), is obviously an educated man skilled in composing in his native language, Greek. Both of his books exhibit a finely attuned ability to communicate well, and they form a genuine history.120 Further, he stands as a second or third generation believer. How so? Because he writes of others, not himself, as âeyewitnesses, and ministers of the wordâ (1:2). Plainly, he looks backward in time to the life and ministry of his Lord. Early Christian tradition puts his residence in Antioch, far to the north of ancient Palestine, in the rather late, so-called âAnti-Marcionite Prologueâ to Lukeâs Gospel. But this information is open to question.121 This said, can we know anything more about him?

The Apostle Paul offers a substantial hint in his epistle to the Colossians about who Luke is: âLuke, the beloved physician, and Demas, greet youâ (Col. 4:14; see also 2 Tim. 4:11; Philem. 1:24). From every appearance, including especially the âweâ passages in Acts, Luke accompanies Paul for extended periods of time (see Acts 16:10â17; 20:5â21:18; 27:1â28:16). Hence, it is natural to see him with Paul when he writes the Colossians letter while a captive in Rome122 or, possibly, in Ephesus123 (see Col. 4:3, 10, 18; also 1:24). Indeed, we know that Luke spends time with Paul while he is a prisoner in Rome (see Acts 28:16); and he may well enjoy a previous acquaintance with âthe elders of the churchâ in Ephesus who come to Miletus at Paulâs request (Acts 20:16â18). Hence, Paul possesses opportunity to mention Luke as if he is known to church members in Colossae.

But do these observations hold up to scrutiny? The primary protest against Lukeâs companionship with Paul arises from the observation that the theology of Paulâs speeches recorded in Acts do not accord with the theological viewpoints in his epistles. But the evidence is mixed. First of all, Luke does not set out to chronicle Paulâs correspondence, or to summarize it. Second, Paulâs impromptu speeches will surely differ from his carefully thought-out letters. Third, the corpus of Paulâs letters will not be gathered until about AD 90, perhaps a decade or more after Luke pens his Gospel and book of Acts. Such a collection, of course, will influence how anyone writes about Paulâs activities. But Luke does not fall under such an influence.124

The more focused question has to do with whether Lukeâs Gospel shows evidence of a physicianâs touch. At the outset, we take notice of H. J. Cadburyâs study which demonstrates that apparent instances of medical vocabulary in the Gospel mirror the language in ancient, nonmedical works.125 Hence, vocabulary by itself, such as the term for leprosy (Greek lepra), does not show Luke to be a physician (see 5:12â13; the Notes thereon). When it comes to possible interest in medical matters, some passages show Lukeâs elevated concern and other passages do not. We offer four examples of each.

Standing as evidence for Lukeâs physician background are the following. First, when Jesus heals Simon Peterâs mother-in-law of a fever, Luke characterizes the illness as âa great feverâ (4:38), a detail that a physician will notice but is missing from Markâs story (see the Notes on 4:35, 38; Mark 1:30â31). Second, in narrating Jesusâ restoration of the withered hand of the man in a synagogue, Luke writes that it is his âright hand [that] was witheredâ (6:6), an observation that a physician will mark, whereas Mark preserves no such remark (see Mark 3:1). Third, Luke notes that the âright earâ of the high priestâs servant is cut off at the time of Jesusâ arrest (see 22:50; also John 18:10) whereas Mark writes no such detail (see Mark 14:47). Fourth, Luke alone notes that, on the spot, Jesusâ heals the ear of this servant (see 22:51; the Notes on 22:50â51). In sum, Luke plainly makes observations that we expect a physician to make.126 But is this the whole story?

On the side that Luke is not a physician, we note the following instances. First, when writing about Jesus healing Peterâs mother-in-law, Luke omits Markâs expression about Jesus taking her by âthe hand,â a seemingly odd omission for a physician (4:39; see the Note thereon; Mark 1:31). Second, in contrast to Markâs detail that the woman with âan issue of bloodâ âfelt in her body that she was healed of that plague,â Luke leaves out her sense that her whole body is healed after touching Jesusâ garment (see 8:44â45; Mark 5:25, 29). Third, according to Luke, Jesus asks a father to lead his afflicted boy to him for a cure (Greek verb prosagÅ), implying his ability to walk (see 9:41), whereas according to Mark the boy is carried (Greek verb pherÅ), a curious adjustment given a physicianâs interest in the condition of a patient (Mark 9:20).127 Fourth, Matthew records that, when Jesus heals the blind men at Jericho (Matthew pictures two blind men), Jesus âhad compassion on them, and touched their eyes,â details that a physician will surely pay attention to but do not appear in Lukeâs account (see 18:42; Matt. 20:34).

Naturally, the strength of the negative observations depends in large part on the relationship of Lukeâs Gospel especially to Markâs Gospel and, to a lesser degree, to Matthewâs report. As we see in our earlier review, the connections are not necessarily robust (see sections III.G and III.H). Nevertheless, Lukeâs interest in medical matters, if he is a physician, does not push itself prominently into view in his Gospel narrative.

C. Geography and Topography

Above, we lay out the case for Luke spending time in Jerusalem when he accompanies the Apostle Paul to the city, remaining at least two weeks and perhaps much longer. Hence, he enjoys an opportunity to see the topography of the area as well as the relationship of parts of the city to their surroundings (see section II.E). That is certainly the case with Bethphage, which lies on the route to Jerusalem from Jericho (see 19:28â29). He also knows that a person can enter the temple directly from the east and north without going through the city (see 19:45).128

The more pressing and interesting question is whether Luke knows the geography of Galilee in the north.129 Even though he evidently spends much or all of two years with Paul in Caesarea, a seaside city (see Acts 27:1â2),130 we do not know whether he travels into the area of Jesusâ youth and early ministry. Most scholars simply dismiss Lukeâs understanding of Galilean topographical features, claiming that his account of events in the north is vague at best. But is this the whole story? Do we find elements that point to Lukeâs firmer grasp of the area? A few examples turn our gaze in a different direction.

First of all, Luke knows of the long descent from Nazareth to Capernaum (see 4:31). From some source, perhaps his own knowledge, he understands that the Sea of Galilee and its shoreline towns lie well below the hilltop village of Nazareth (see the Note on 4:31). In this connection, moreover, Luke knows the direction of the wind that rakes across the lake during a stormâit rushes âdownâ to the surface from the surrounding heights (8:23). Matthew and Mark preserve no such notation (see Matt. 8:24; Mark 4:37). So they are not Lukeâs source (see the Note on 8:23). Furthermore, in the account of Jesusâ trip to the east side of the lake where he heals a demoniac, Luke adds the expression âover against Galileeâ to clarify the locale of miracle (8:26). Again, neither Matthew nor Mark carry a notation with this kind of specificity (see the Note on 8:26; Matt. 8:28; Mark 5:1). In addition, Luke consciously situates the stories of Jesusâ long journey to Jerusalem into a region generally south of Galilee (see 9:51â19:40), rimming them along the imprecise border with Samaria (see 9:52â53; 17:11; see the Notes on 9:52 and 17:11; the Analysis on 9:51â56; 17:11â19).

Above all, indicators of Lukeâs basic grasp of Galilean topography rise within the setting for Jesusâ Sermon on the Plain. Throughout the night before the sermon, Jesus prays on âa mountain.â In the morning, from his followers he selects the âtwelveâ whom he names âapostles,â and brings them with him to âthe plainâ where he will speak to âa great multitudeâ (6:12â13, 17). For those who hold that Jesusâ Sermon on the Plain and Sermon on the Mount are variant versions of the same sermon, the notation about âthe plainâ creates a problem because no such place exists along the northern shore of the Sea of Galilee, the evident place where Jesus delivers his Sermon on the Mount. For here the ground slopes up from the shoreline for a short distance before rising steeply into the surrounding heights. But a completely different topography presents itself on the northwest corner of the lake, a mere three miles from Capernaum (see the Notes on 6:12, 17). There, setting off a rather level place, mountains and cliffs surround an extended plain that reaches inland and westward about a mile from the edge of the lake. This spot matches exactly Lukeâs topographic description of land forms for Jesusâ calling of the Twelve and for his sermon (see the Analysis on 6:20â49). Furthermore, this plain is the natural place for hearers from âTyre and Sidonâ to arrive from the north and west because the road for them comes down through the Arbel Pass onto this level area (see the Note on 6:17).131 In fact, if Luke indeed visits Nazareth and Capernaum, he travels this same road.

Reasons for seeing Luke unacquainted with Galilean geography include the following. First, he omits Capernaum as the place for Jesusâ healing of the paralyzed man who is carried on a stretcher (5:17; see Mark 2:1; also Matt. 9:1). If Luke knows Markâs account, the omission is curious unless he has some other purpose in mind, such as universalizing Jesusâ action by separating it from its geographical context (see the Note on 5:17). Second, he leaves out the location Caesarea Philippi for Peterâs confession of Jesus as âThe Christ of God,â a geographical remark preserved in the other synoptic Gospels (see 9:18â20; Matt. 16:13; Mark 8:27). In this case, the city of Caesarea Philippi lies far to the north of the Sea of Galilee and, if Luke visits the Galilee area, he very possibly does not travel far from the lake. After all, nothing distinctive from Jesusâ ministry occurs between Caesarea Philippi and the environs of the Sea of Galilee. Third, Luke neglects pointing to Jesusâ ministry âbeyond Jordan,â that is, on the east bank of the river (Matt. 19:1; Mark 10:1). Instead, he inserts rather vague notices of Jesusâ travel with disciples after departing Galilee (see the Notes on 9:51; 10:38; 14:25; 17:11; 18:35; 19:28). But much of this travel runs along and through the Jordan Valley. If Luke, in fact, comes to Galilee from Caesarea on the Mediterranean coast, he will surely return the same way rather than roaming through the Jordan Valley, taking a very roundabout and climatologically hot route back to the Mediterranean Sea which will have schooled him in the difficulty of the ascent to Jerusalem from Jericho, a climb that he does not seem to appreciate (see the Note on 19:28). In sum, we conclude that none of these omissions rule out the distinct possibility that Luke possesses a basic acquaintance of Galilean geography from his own visit.

One final observation is in order. Although all the Gospels preserve the miracle of the feeding of the five thousand, Luke is the only writer to identify its locale: âa desert [uninhabited] place belonging to the city called Bethsaidaâ (9:10). How does he know this fact? The most natural response is that he learns it from someone who earlier is present, either from one of the early disciples or from one residing in the area. Because Bethsaida sits near the north shore of the Sea of Galilee a mere three miles east of Capernaum, a place easily reached by boat or by foot (see Matt. 14:13; Mark 6:32â33; John 6:17), and because from other clues noted above Luke likely visits this shoreline region, he is evidently in a firm position to point to the location of this remarkable event (see the Note on 9:10).

20:T328d8,

Enthroned above all creation towers the exalted, glorified Christ. Descending into the darkest recesses of human agony and sin reaches the warm, caring Jesus. These two are the same person. Lukeâs testimony introduces us to this man become GodâGod the Son. Not that this man is a mere man. No. As Luke emphasizes when presenting this person, he comes into our world already bearing a divine nature, already carrying divine qualities. Thus he arrives at his birth as Savior, as âChrist the Lordâ (2:11).1 But he does not stride through mortality without challenges, both mental and physical. At one moment during his ministry, as he contemplates his future suffering, he declares, âI have a baptism to be baptized with; and how am I straitened [= distressed] till it be accomplished!â (12:50). Months later, it is apparent that he has resolved his fears and worries when âhe went beforeâ the crowd of his disciples, pushing the pace on the long, steep climb from Jericho to Jerusalem, ready to face his foreordained destiny in the capital city (see the Note on 19:28).

I. Character of This Commentary

The most distinguishing element of this line-by-line commentary is the introduction of distinctive Latter-day Saint scriptures to cast light on various passages in Lukeâs Gospel. Those scriptural sources consist of the Book of Mormon, the Doctrine and Covenants (abbreviated D&C), and the Pearl of Great Price. The Book of Mormon recites the story of a family that departs Jerusalem in the early sixth century BC and makes its way to the New World by first traveling through Arabia and then sailing across the ocean, thereafter founding a civilization that becomes fractured. The Doctrine and Covenants includes revelations and inspired statements from Joseph Smith, the founding prophet of the Latter-day Saint movement, and from a few of his successors. Notably, it is here that we run into the term testimony to describe one of the New Testament Gospels (see D&C 88:3, 141). The Pearl of Great Price collects a number of items that come from the hand of Joseph Smith, including his own story about early revelatory events that impacted his life and the lives of his followers.

It is not surprising, therefore, that the commentary features elements of Lukeâs Gospel that interest Latter-day Saints, although it focuses primarily on topics that arise in the text. For example, the gap in Lukeâs record concerning the Saviorâs activities in the world of departed spirits while his body lies in the tomb is of particular interest to Latter-day Saints because an important set of doctrines arises from his activity there (see section II.G below). Moreover, Latter-day Saints emphasize the sacred and enduring nature of the family. This commentary highlights passage after passage that feature the importance of the family (see section II.B below).

Naturally, I have consulted a cluster of notable commentaries that have appeared before mine, written by scholars with viewpoints different from my own. These include Alfred Plummerâs The Gospel according to S. Luke (1989 impression), I. Howard Marshallâs The Gospel of Luke: A Commentary on the Greek Text (1978), Joseph A. Fitzmyerâs The Gospel According to Luke in two volumes (1981, 1985), Leon Morrisâs Luke: An Introduction and Commentary (2d edition, 1988), Joel B. Greenâs The Gospel of Luke (1997), and Raymond E. Brownâs The Birth of the Messiah (1979) and The Death of the Messiah in two volumes (1994). One of my heroes is the nine-volume Theological Dictionary of the New Testament, edited by Gerhard Kittel and Gerhard Friedrich and published in English between 1964 and 1974. The thorough and thoughtful character of these works has drawn me to them. And I readily acknowledge my dependence.

This commentary rests on the language of the King James Translation of the Bible. This text is the standard for English-speaking members of the Latter-day Saint faith. All discussions begin with this translation. Paired with the KJV, as it is called, is a fresh translation that the editors of the commentary series call a New Rendition, prepared to illustrate how a Greek text can be understood a little differently and how it can be rendered into modern English.

For transliterating Greek and Hebrew terms, I have relied on the transliteration standards adopted by the Society of Biblical Literature.

Finally, I have written the commentary mostly in the present tense because, in my view, it is much more vivid than the past tense. Naturally, I have also written occasionally in the perfect tense because it describes action that comes into the present. Hence, the perfect tense links naturally to the present. Ultimately, readers will decide whether by this means I succeed at making the world of Jesus and his actions and teachings more accessible. Importantly, all of my Notes and Analyses are based on the language of the King James Translation of the Bible, not on the accompanying rendition.

II. Portrait of the Savior

A. Jesusâ Compassion

More than all other Gospel accounts, except that preserved in 3 Nephi 11â26, Luke captures the compassion and love of the Savior. Such sweet concern manifests itself particularly for the downtrodden and those forced to the margins of society. Within this frame, most notable is Jesusâ compassion for women and children, a compassion that springs into view in story after story. This aspect of Lukeâs Gospel does not derive from some special interest on his part nor from his sources. Jesusâ compassion brims over again and again, a dimension of his life that is truly genuine and cannot be hidden. For instance, it manifests itself in his declaration about divorce, that gives back dignity to women caught in its web (see the Note on 16:18), and in his heartfelt appeal to the women and children of Jerusalem as he is led to the cross (see 23:27â31; the Analysis on 23:27â33). As a companion dimension, when setting the stage for his report, Luke introduces us to Elisabeth and Mary, who are recipients of Godâs gracious acts, of Godâs compassion both for them and, through them, for the rest of humankind. For Godâs love is bundled into the arrival of their expected children and into their respective ministries. In sum, within these stories, Luke discloses the deep, divine love that runs through his narrative of the Christ.

Further examples will illustrate. Initially Jesus himself signals his interest in merciful moments, particularly as they affect women and children, when he cites the story of the widow of Sarepta and her son whom the prophet Elijah delivers from the groping grasp of famine (see 4:25â26). In Lukeâs second recorded miracle, Jesus heals Simonâs mother-in-law of a fever, restoring full strength to her on the spot so that she âimmediately . . . arose and ministered unto [Simon and his guests]â (4:39). A few weeks later, he leads his growing band of followers up to the town of Nain and rescues an unnamed widow from a bleak future by raising her only child, a grown son, from death. His efforts to reach her village and meet the funeral procession as it moves toward the cemetery stand as a witness of his love for this otherwise unknown woman and, of course, her only son (see 7:11â16). Later, Jesusâ kind and generous response to the acts of a âsinnerâ woman while he is a dinner guest in the home of a Phariseeâcomplimenting her in the presence of his host and other guests and then publicly forgiving her sinsâunderline not only his wish to make a point about how the men gathered in that home should treat women but especially his desire to bring relief to her (see 7:36â50).

Luke shares with the other Synoptic writers, Matthew and Mark, the twin stories of the healing of the woman with the twelve-year issue of blood and the raising of the young daughter of Jairus from the dead (see 8:41â56; Matt. 9:18â26; Mark 5:21â43). Remarkably, in Lukeâs retelling, Jesus allows the woman to explain to those present, most of whom know her and her situation, what she has done and what results from her touch of his garment. Now, in a brief moment, all of them know that she is healed, that she is no longer ritually impure and physically impaired, and that she can rejoin them as a full member of their community, no longer obliged to stand off by herself. In a single stroke, she is healed religiously, physically, and socially.

In three other instances, Jesus steps forward to act rather than responding to a verbal request. First, in a synagogue, he offers healing to a woman âbowed togetherâ for âeighteen years,â leading âthe ruler of the synagogueâ to heap âindignationâ on him because it is the Sabbath. But Jesus holds firm, knowing that this woman needs relief (13:10â17). Second, in a dinner setting, a man âwhich had the dropsyâ approaches him as he reclines at the table (14:2). Jesus rises, walks around the table, then âtook him, and healed him.â The plain sense is that Jesus takes him in his arms and offers healing (see the Note on 14:4). Third, as we note above, when he is forced to walk toward the cross, he turns to the women in the gathered crowd who are lamenting his fate. In poignant tones, he warns them about their future: âDaughters of Jerusalem, weep not for me, but weep for yourselves, and for your children.â Why? Because terrible âdays are comingâ (23:27â29). His heart aches for them.

Not surprisingly, women from Galilee are the first witnesses to the Saviorâs resurrection. Besides discovering the empty tomb, they meet two angels who effectively send them as emissaries to the other followers still in town (see 24:1â11). Strikingly, according to the angels, these women are among those who hear Jesus predict his passion while in Galilee. The angelsâ words thus clarify that the women are part of the larger group of disciples who travel with Jesus (see 24:6â7; also 9:22, 44; the Note on 8:2). More remarkably, the naming of the women at 24:10 matches an almost identical list at 8:2â3 and forms an inclusio of sorts that not only underscores the unity of Lukeâs Gospel2 but also ranks these women almost on a par with the Twelve as witnesses who âhave companied with us all the time that the Lord Jesus went in and out among usâ (Acts 1:21).

It is not out of some special interest on Lukeâs part that early in his narrative, as we just noticed, he singles out women who are joining the ranks of Jesusâ disciples (see 8:2â3). Their attraction to his teachings and miracles is as genuine as that of the men. This universalism, regularly noticed by commentators, fits snugly with the passage that Luke quotes from Isaiah 40 about the Baptistâs ministry, âall flesh shall see the salvation of Godâ (3:6; LXX Isa. 40:5). Further, the presence of women among Jesusâ followers bears eloquent testimony to the love and concern that they feel flowing from him to them. His genuine compassion draws them to him, notable because among these women are some who come from lofty positions in society. Luke notes one of these, âJoanna the wife of Chuza Herodâs stewardâ (8:3). Importantly, Joannaâs discipleship illustrates not only Jesusâ reach across the social and economic spectrum of his day but also his targeted inclusion of women.

Two omissions are curious. In Jesusâ statement about those who are his spiritual family, specifically his mother and brothers, he omits the term âsisterâ that sits in the other accounts of this scene (see Matt. 12:50; Mark 3:35). Either his source does not preserve the word or, if he knows Markâs version of the report, Luke chooses to let the term âmotherâ carry the weight of dignity for women (see the Note on 8:21). Luke also omits an entire episode preserved by Mark and Matthew about Jesusâ quiet trip to the region of Tyre and Sidon, cities that lie north of Galilee on the Mediterranean Sea. This account features Jesusâ interaction with a gentile woman who begs for his help in driving an unclean spirit from her daughter (see Matt. 15:21â28; Mark 7:24â30). But importantly, this story falls within a long section of Markâs Gospel from which Luke adopts nothing (see Mark 6:45â8:26). Knowing of Lukeâs interest in repeating reports of Jesusâ interaction with women, we are left with the explanation that he includes only accounts in his Gospel that add a distinctively important characteristic of Jesus for his readers. He does not record stories simply to multiply them without purpose (see the Analysis on 9:18â22).

A twin track that highlights Jesusâ loving concern for sinners appears in Lukeâs record. The accounts are so varied and so numerous that we must reckon Jesusâ compassion for such people as a well-established facet of his ministry. As background, his society labels a variety of people as sinners, including people who lead an immoral life or whose occupations might draw them into dishonesty, such as tax collectors, peddlers, donkey drivers, prostitutes, gamblers, and shepherds.3

Jesus regularly and often pushes through the social barriers that people in his society erect against persons of such backgrounds. This stereotypical labeling is most clearly seen in his story of the Pharisee and the publican wherein the Pharisee places himself on a plane far above the publican and others whom he characterizes as âextortioners, unjust, adulterersâ and, disdainfully, âthis publican,â with a hissing emphasis on âthisâ (18:11). Jesusâ compassionate view of the publican, of course, differs markedly, declaring him to be âjustifiedâ because of his humility (see the Notes on 18:9â14).

Jesusâ outreach to such people appears early in Lukeâs narrative at the calling of Levi, a customs collector whose office sits just east of Capernaum. Although this man is honorable, as his call from the Savior demonstrates, he is peevishly associated with âpublicans and sinnersâ in the minds of those who are acquainted with him. When scribes and Pharisees complain to Jesusâ disciples instead of to him about the company he keeps, Jesus answers with a classic response: âThey that are whole need not a physician; but they that are sick. I came not to call the righteous, but sinners to repentanceâ (5:27â32). In a later scene, it becomes clear that over the course of Jesusâ ministry he gains a reputation for constant association with sinners, even sharing meals with them, the worst of all possible acts (see 15:1â2).

As we have seen above, Jesusâ interaction with the âsinnerâ woman at a dinner in a Phariseeâs home brims with warmth. As readers, we readily grasp his concern for this woman who comes to him in faith, seeking forgiveness for her sins. She obviously learns of his power to forgive from some earlier occasion, perhaps that of the paralyzed man carried to Jesus on a stretcher (see 5:18â26). Because her reputation is known among the guests at the meal, Jesus offers forgiveness to her in a public wayââThy sins are forgiven,â he intones in their presence (7:48)âso that these people are witnesses of her new status before God. Significantly, although he alludes to her soiled past, he respectfully does not call her a sinner (see 7:36â50).

A final report involves the chief tax collector in Jericho, Zacchaeus. This small man is so detested by people in the town that, when he tries to get a glimpse of Jesus and his entourage, they close ranks so that he cannot see above their shoulders. And when Jesus pays attention to him by calling him out of his perch in a tree, townspeople complain noisily that Jesus is going âto be [a] guest with a man that is a sinner.â But the Savior does not recoil. Instead, in an unprecedented act, he spends that night and likely the next with this man and his family, bringing blessings into their home (see the Notes on 19:1â10).

As is now clear, the whole of Lukeâs Gospel overflows with the Saviorâs tender concern for these people. But this compassion is not merely a part of some social program that Jesus aims to institute. To be sure, he seeks to integrate repentant sinners into his kingdom. Yet this is only a part of the larger picture. Out of his continued association with such people, a strong sense emerges that the messianic era has arrived and is now present in the person of Jesus. His deepest, sweetest concern is with repentant sinners (see 15:7). God can forgive these penitent people and offer salvation to them (see 7:50). This frames a major reason that Jesus associates with and receives sinners.4

B. Home and Family

One of the most enduring features of Lukeâs Gospel is his emphasis on the importance of families. In the view of some, the topic is completely exhausted by two of Jesusâ sayings. He utters the first when his mother and brothers come to visit him and he then says to a gathering, âMy mother and my brethren are these which hear the word of God, and do itâ (8:20â21). On a second occasion he declares, âIf any man come to me, and hate not his father, and mother, and wife, and children, and brethren, and sisters, . . . he cannot be my discipleâ (14:26), understanding the verb âto hateâ in the sense of âto turn against whatever distracts from primary loyaltiesâ (see the Note on 14:26).5 But is this the end of the matter? The answer is a resounding No. The themes of home and family run deep in Lukeâs account. We cannot review everything here, but we can highlight some of the most important passages, leaving the rest in the Notes and Analysis sections (see also the introduction to chapter 14).6

Luke opens his narrative with a married couple, Zacharias and Elisabeth. Such an opening adds a layer of meaning to his record, a meaning that brings focus onto the family. According to the story, with heavenly aid, this older man and woman become parents in a miraculous way, completing their family when they welcome a son into their home. The appearance of the angel Gabriel to Zacharias the priest in the templeâs sanctuary begins the eventful actions. But they quickly move to Zachariasâ home. During a period of months, that home becomes a spiritual reservoir, a family space where Godâs majesty becomes visible in the lives of this man and woman. Against all odds, Elisabeth becomes pregnant and gives birth to a son. Meanwhile, to that home comes the young Mary, who is already carrying her divine infant. At the moment the two women meet, at Elisabethâs home, the spirit of God pours down upon them in a rush of celestial grace and knowledge, allowing Elisabeth to share with Mary one of the secrets of the ages: who the mother of the Messiah is to beâa stunning revelation to Elisabeth that occurs inside her home. Then, at the naming and circumcision of her child, her husband, Zacharias, is miraculously released from his divinely imposed inabilities to hear and speak, and, led by the spirit of God, he prophesies about his infant sonâs service as the Messiahâs herald.7 This entire set of scenes is family-centered.

The next revelation, after the one to Zacharias in the temple, comes to Mary, inside her home. Importantly, Lukeâs language leads us within her parentsâ home: he writes that âthe angel came in unto herâ (1:28 and the Note thereon). More than this, the Greek text agrees that the angel enters an enclosed space.8 Maryâs surprise at the presence of a manâthe angelâstrengthens this observation, for the angel appears where she evidently thinks she is safe from intruders. At first, she is âtroubledâ when the angel appears; he responds, âFear not, Mary,â addressing directly her fear and surprise (1:29â30). Obviously, she is in a comfortable place, at least for her, and she does not expect a visitor in that spot. All indicators say that she is in her parentsâ home. If so, the angel comes into the space where family living takes place, subtly underscoring the home and family as the place of spiritual nourishment and instruction.

When we examine Jesusâ ministry, in one of his early miracles he cleanses a leper (5:12â14). If we look beyond the miracle, we see the manâs family figuratively standing off to the side. Luke records that the man is âfull of leprosy,â probably an advanced stage of the illness (5:12). We are obviously looking at a man who has borne his affliction for a number of years, his disease growing worse with time. This observation means that he and his family enjoy almost no contact with one another throughout much of that period. Not coincidentally, he is banned from places of gathering and worship. But in one transforming moment, Jesus restores this man to his family and home, to the synagogue and the temple, healing him socially, religiously, and physically, just as he does for the woman with the issue of blood (see 8:43â48). Among the most prized possessions that now come back to the man are his family and his home (see the Note on 5:12).

When we observe others of Jesusâ miracles, we come to the same point. In each case, whether the healing of the paralyzed man (see 5:18â25), the raising of the only son of the widow in Nain (see 7:11â15), the freeing of the Gergesene demoniac (see 8:26â39), or the raising of Jairusâs daughter (8:41â42, 49â56), these beneficiaries of Jesusâ healing powersâand othersâcome from homes and families whose members have to adjust to the needs and limitations of the afflicted person. When these individuals return whole to their respective families, family members suddenly have a burden lifted from their shoulders. In making individuals whole, Jesus makes homes and families whole in a new way. Does he know this? Of course.

When we turn attention to Jesusâ teachings, we quickly discover how many touch directly on families and homes. Stepping aside from the obvious, such as the parable of the prodigal son, we turn to the account of Mary and Martha, a experience that occurs inside their home. An additional theme of prayer emerges from this report. Martha and Mary are hosting Jesus for a special meal. When Martha pleads with Jesus to encourage Mary help her with meal preparations, she is essentially begging or âprayingâ for his help. Jesusâ response, a divine response to her plea for help we must remember, comes to her in her home, pointing to the home setting as a proper place of divine instruction (see 10:38â42). Does she grasp his message and implement it in her life? The answer is Yes. The end of the story comes in Johnâs Gospel when, many months later, Martha walks to meet Jesus on the road into Bethany and he reveals to her, by herself, one of the grand keys of the universe: âI am the resurrection, and the life: he that believeth in me, though he were dead, yet shall he liveâ (John 11:25). By this time in her life, she is ready to receive such a truth.

The two themes, prayer and home, carry into chapter 11. At the request of his disciples to âteach us to prayâ (11:1), Jesus turns attention to this practice, featuring families in part of his answer (see 11:1â13; the Analysis on 11:5â10). Further comments on family and home arise from critics who claim that Jesus casts out âdevils through Beelzebub the chief of the devilsâ (11:15). Their critique leads to a most interesting reply wherein Jesus appeals to three images in quick succession, all having to do with family and home (see the Analysis on 11:14â28).

In an astute statement, Jesus first draws attention to a divided kingdom: âEvery kingdom divided against itself is brought to desolation,â he intones (11:17). Jesus then points to a house that leans against another house, implying either bad foundations or sloppy construction methods. Significantly, a second meaning of Jesusâ picture of the tottering house has to do with damaging divisions within a family when we understand the term âhouseâ to mean household or family, which the Greek term oikos and Hebrew bayit often signify.9 Understood in this way, Jesusâ statement encompasses the unwelcome prospect of enfeebling family divisions, which he here warns against (see 11:17â20).

Jesus next leaps to the image of âa strong man.â When the strong manâs palace, or his home, is secure and âhis goods are in peaceâ (11:21), all must be well at home and in the surrounding countryside. But matters quickly turn upside down when a person âstronger than heâ assails him and, in the ensuing melee, pushes him out of his house and other property. All commentators agree that, because Jesus is here warning about the devil, the strong man is the devil and the stronger is himself. That said, what do we learn? Jesusâ image implies that the devil can settle into a home and remain in charge until the Lord shows up, forces him out and frees his âgoods,â that is, his captive souls (Greek ta hyparchonta).10 Not incidentally, if we grasp that Jesus speaks both on a terrestrial level and on a celestial level, frequently at the same time, we here come upon one of the few allusions in the Gospels to his future work with departed spirits (see 11:21â23; the Notes on 11:21â22).

In a third turn, Jesus draws his criticsâ attention to an image of âthe unclean spiritâ who walks âthrough dry places, seeking rest.â When this spirit finds no place to rest, it returns to âmy houseâ (11:24; note the possessive) and finds the house âswept and garnished.â It then recruits âseven other spirits more wicked than himselfâ and settles back into his former house. Jesus declares that âthe last state of that man is worse than the firstâ (11:25â26). What is the lesson? A home thoroughly âswept and garnishedâ fails as a familyâs refuge from harmful influences if family members put nothing in place to resist wickedness, or to take the place of earlier enticing evil (see 11:24â26).

In all of these illustrations from chapter 11, Jesusâ attention rests on the home and those who reside in it. Although one thrust of these verses brings forward the Saviorâs power over the world of demons, he intriguingly binds his demon-defeating power to the dimension of home and households. Frighteningly, lying amidst the banter of Jesus with his opponents and rising within his responses to them, the home stands close to the world of evil, so close, in fact, that the world of evil both forms a crippling intrusion into the home and, in Jesusâ words, must be overcome and separated from it. To say it another way, without a victorious effort from the good, evil will make its permanent camp within our homes. In sum, the illustrations and sayings packed into these verses illuminate Jesusâ concern for the home, underlining its proper place as an intended sanctuary from evil, obviously with his help as the âstrongerâ one.

A second teaching arises within Jesusâ Sermon on the Plain, at the end of which we hear his raised voice. âWhosoever cometh to me, and heareth my sayings, and doeth them, I will shew you to whom he is like.â He goes on to elaborate: âHe is like a man which built an house, and digged deep, and laid the foundation on a rock: and when the flood arose, the stream beat vehemently upon that house, and could not shake it: for it was founded upon a rockâ (6:47â48). The image of building a house, which Jesus appeals to here and elsewhere (see 20:17; Matt. 7:24â27; 3 Ne. 11:39; 14:24â27), is one that warns a builder to be aware of seasonal rains and floods that wash through river valleys. While most commentators focus on the type of house that Jesusâ words envision, the more important element for our purposes has to do with the inhabitants of the house, that is, the household or family (Greek oikia here; Hebrew bayit). Understanding the terms in their figurative sense, Jesus declares that a parent is obliged to create a home that rests on a sure foundation, on bedrock, which foundation is himself (see the Notes on 6:48â49; the Analysis on 6:20â49).11

Let us take up an example of Luke not knowing the whole story, as I reconstruct the matter, or at least he does not record it. It has to do with the call of Peter and Andrew, James and John (see 5:1â11).12 A second instance, which we shall not review here, concerns the chief tax collector in Jericho, Zacchaeus, and his family (on this latter report, see the Note on 19:5; the Analysis on 19:1â10).13

Most readers know the story of Jesus commandeering Peterâs boat when pressed by a crowd at the Sea of Galilee near Capernaum. Jesus sits down in the boat and speaks to the crowd on the shore while Peter listens as he works on his nets. At the end of this sermon, the crowd walks away and Jesus instructs Peter to row farther out on the lake and to âlet down [his] nets for a draughtâ (5:4). Peter resists: âMaster, we have toiled all the night, and have taken nothing.â However, in the end he agrees that, âat thy word I will let down the netâ (5:5). Peter strings out the net and it fills with fish, so many that his net begins to break. We can almost see some of the twisted twine begin to unravel and we can nearly hear other strands pop as they snap. Peter and his brother Andrew, who is in the boat, a fact secured by the plural pronouns in this passage, yell to their partners, James and John, to come and help them with the catch. When they finally muscle the fish into the two boats, the boats âbegan to sinkâ (5:7).

At this moment, Peter falls âat Jesusâ knees, saying, Depart from me; for I am a sinful man, O Lordâ (5:8). Peter senses that he is in the presence of the Divine after seeing Jesus cast out a devil from a man in the synagogue a day or two earlier (see 4:33â37), then witnessing Jesus heal his mother-in-law following the synagogue service (see 4:38â39), next hearing Jesusâ sermon delivered from his own boat, and now, with Jesusâ help, hauling in the huge catch of fish. His partners come to the same sense. Here Luke records tersely that âwhen they had brought their ships to land, they forsook all, and followed himâ (5:11).

But we have to ask, What about all those fish? Do we believe that Jesus performs this miracle mainly to impress these men? Asked another way, does Jesus perform the miracle only to let all those fish go to waste? Will lifelong fishermen allow all those fish to go to waste, men who know their value for food and for income? A lot of fish lie in those two boats if they are anything close to the size of a first-century boat found in the mud along the northern shore of the Sea of Galilee in 1986. That boat measures 27 feet long, 7.5 feet wide, and is 4.3 feet deep, from gunwale to keel.14

Let us return to our question. Does Jesus spend divine power on a miracle that leaves a lot of waste? We observe, first, that the fish will feed the families of the departing men, and will give family members a little income if they sell some of the fish at the market. But that help will last a maximum of forty-eight hours. After that, the fish will spoil. These observations and the prior question put us on the horns of a dilemma. My response begins with the ancient geographer Strabo (64 BCâAD 21). When he discusses the Sea of Galilee, he notes a fish salting industry at a place called Taricheae, some five miles or so southwest of Capernaum.15 Importantly, this name comes from the Greek term tarichos, which means âdried or smoked fish.â16 With an effort to sail or row the two boats to this town, Peter and his partners will preserve this massive haul of fish for months. As fishermen they know the town and the service that is available there. Letâs credit their good sense to preserve this unusually large catch. Surprisingly, this part of the story remains untold (see the Notes on 5:6, 11; 9:33; 19:5).

In addition, Jesus knows that he is calling breadwinners away from their wives and children, and he graciously provides for the needs of these family members, both for sustenance and for income. In the long view, the miracle of the fish is not simply for show, a point that he makes with the devil (see 4:9â12). It carries a noble and important purpose, the sustaining of families. This nurturing care for the families of the Twelve is clothed in words from modern scripture: âI, the Lord, give unto [the Twelve of modern times] a promise that I will provide for their familiesâ (D&C 118:3). Thus, with an unexpected turn, Jesus brings families to stand at the center of his gracious, miraculous acts (see the Note on 5:6).

Another family-centered stream runs through the Gospel. At base, it has to do with honoring parents, whether as a youth who obeys them or as an adult who cares for them.17 The commandment comes up in the conversation between Jesus and the rich ruler. The ruler begins by asking Jesus, âWhat shall I do to inherit eternal life?â Jesus responds by saying, âThou knowest the commandments.â He then mentions five of the Ten Commandments, ending with, âHonor thy father and thy motherâ (18:18, 20). Although we do not know the situation of the rulerâs parents, Jesus clearly expects him to see to their welfare (see the Note on 18:20).

Not surprisingly, the earliest example of a child honoring parents is Jesus himself. After finding the youthful Jesus in the temple, Mary and Joseph return with him to their home in Nazareth where âhe was subject unto themâ (2:51). It is plain that he submits willingly. A second instance arises in his parable of the wicked husbandmen that he recounts near the end of his life (see 20:9â16). The key element in the story has to do with the beloved sonâs obedience to his father when he already knows how the husbandmen have dealt with the three servants whom his father has sent to collect the rent (see the Notes on 2:51; 20:13).

On the other side, that of children taking care of parents, we notice two instances. The first stands as an example of honoring a parent. After a synagogue service in Capernaum, Jesus comes to the home of Peter where they find Peterâs mother-in-law suffering from a fever (see 4:38â39). The significant part for our interests is the fact that this woman, the mother of Peterâs wife, is in their home. Although we do not know what circumstances bring her to their home, it seems evident that Peter and his wife have welcomed her and are caring for her. The second example consists of both neglect and responsibility. We find them in the parable of the prodigal son (see 15:11â32). As the story unfolds, we see the younger son cutting his ties to his parents, deftly and purposely avoiding any obligation to care for them in their old age. In contrast, the older son stays with his parents, working hard to make the estate productive and laying a firm base for the care of his parents in their old age. Each of these instances points directly at the commandment to honor parents (see the Notes on 4:38; 15:13, 29).18

In summary, a little digging discloses a plethora of instances wherein the concern for families and homes appears at or just beneath the surface of Lukeâs text. Those concerns range from Jesus healing families through healing individuals, to showing his interest in widows and children, the most vulnerable in his society, to his teaching about the sanctity of the home by keeping it free of the devilâs influences, to providing miraculously for the families of his chosen disciples, to stressing the command to honor oneâs father and mother.

C. Doing Good

In a scene unique to Lukeâs Gospel, the Savior lays out the broad agenda for his ministry while standing among acquaintances in the Nazareth synagogue: âThe Spirit of the Lord . . . hath anointed me to preach the gospel to the poor; he hath sent me to heal the brokenhearted, to preach deliverance to the captives, and recovering of sight to the blind, to set at liberty them that are bruisedâ (4:18). His is not a task that leads him into the chambers of power or one that puts him in the coveted company of decision makers in his society. He will go to people who have needs, a feature that drenches the Gospel account and is therefore authentic. Not surprisingly, he expects his followers to fully adopt the same focus.

In two juxtaposed sayings that have to do with widows, Jesus holds up an important thread of what it means to do good, of what it means to be his disciple. In the first, when addressing followers, he warns: âBeware of the scribes, which . . . devour widowsâ housesâ (20:46â47). In the very next scene, when observing a poor widow offering a meager gift of âtwo mitesâ at the temple, he declares that âthis poor widow . . . of her penury hath cast in all the living that she hadâ (21:2â4).

For Jesus, the actions of the scribes are repugnant. Plainly driven by a desire to acquire the goods of this world, they allow that desire to overrule their sense of justice and compassion for a widowâs situation. Although we cannot know precisely the kind of highhanded actions that Jesus has in mind, it is apparent that these men have stepped over a threshold that will carry consequences into eternity. In fact, because of the setting for Jesusâ comments, just before he prophesies about the destruction of Jerusalem (see 21:5â24), his notice of their actions exposes them as part of the evil mix that draws the wrath of God onto the city (see the Analysis on 21:1â4). The point for Jesusâ followers? They must resist the natural tendency to acquire goods when it means trampling on others. Instead, they are âto heal the brokenheartedâ and to assist âthem that are bruisedâ (4:18). What is worse, the scribes behave badly while adorning themselves in the trappings of pietyâwalking âin long robesâ and insisting that they be allowed to sit in âthe highest seats in the synagoguesâ (20:46). Jesusâ disciples are to avoid such behavior at all costs (see the Note on 20:47; the Analysis on 20:45â47).

The poor widow in the temple stands in complete contrast to the scribes and even to the temple authorities. Draped in the clothing of humility, she approaches the offering chests in the Court of the Women bearing âall the living that she hadâ (21:4). We are justified in seeing her action to help others as the highest good. From her act, Jesus is able to draw his disciples into a lesson of ultimate devotion, of ultimate worship, of ultimate discipleship. Her gift severely diminishes her ability to provide for herself. But she gives anyway: âshe of her penury hath cast in all the living that she hadâ (21:4). She is the real disciple, giving all.

Luke often captures Jesusâ good deeds on the Sabbath, deeds that his disciples observe and are expected to follow. We think immediately of him healing the man with the withered right hand in a synagogue (see 6:6â10). As Jesus and the afflicted man stand together in the middle of the room, Jesus turns to look into the faces of all in the gathered congregation and asks the question, âIs it lawful on the sabbath days to do good, or to do evil? to save life, or to destroy it?â (6:9; also 14:3). Which will it be? For him, the Sabbath does not restrict doing good but offers a sacred occasion for helping others. In a brief moment, he turns everyone in the building into witnesses of his goodness even though some are âfilled with madnessâ because they believe that he is desecrating both the Sabbath and the synagogue (6:11).

Similarly, when he finds a woman in a synagogue âwhich had a spirit of infirmity eighteen years, and was bowed together,â he literally reaches out to her. After calling her to where he is seated, he publicly offers relief to her by laying his hands on her and declaring that she is âloosed from [her] infirmity.â As all look on in a breathless silence, âimmediately she was made straight, and glorified Godâ (13:11â13). When the synagogue director, in an effort to draw back the loyalty of the congregation to himself, objects loudly because âJesus had healed on the sabbath day,â Jesus responds by asking, âought not this woman, being a daughter of Abraham, . . . be loosed from this bond on the sabbath day?â (13:14, 16). Clearly, the Sabbath opens opportunities for doing good and, not incidentally, brings people together who witness Jesusâ exemplary actions (see the Analysis on 13:10â17).

But Jesusâ example does not carry his message alone. His teachings also lift into clear view the principle of doing good to others. For instance, in the Sermon on the Plain, his premier teaching in Lukeâs Gospel, he pushes this idea hard: âLove your enemies, do good to them which hate you, Bless them that curse you, and pray for them which despitefully use you. And unto him that smiteth thee on the one cheek offer also the otherâ (6:27â29). As if these words are not enough, he soon adds emphasis by essentially repeating the central theme: âlove ye your enemies, and do goodâ (6:35). But the requirement does not apply just to our mental psyches or our physical persons. It also has to do with our property: âhim that taketh away thy cloke forbid not to take thy coat also. Give to every man that asketh of thee; and of him that taketh away thy goods ask them not againâ (6:29â30). Moments later he runs over similar ground: âlend, hoping for nothing againâ (6:35). In all, Jesus indicates that all such actions will bear eternal rewards: âGive, and it shall be given unto you; good measure, pressed down, and shaken together, and running over. . . . For with the same measure that ye mete withal it shall be measured to you againâ (6:38). The everlasting consequences are that âyour reward shall be great, and ye shall be the children of the Highestâ (6:35).

Notably, just as Jesus is the mortal example of doing good, so especially is his Father from on high: âfor he is kind unto the unthankful and to the evilâ (6:35). The issue for us is whether we are willing to follow the Father and the Son as our standard setters.

Anchoring these teachings are the beloved lines known as the Golden Rule: âas ye would that men should do to you, do ye also to them likewiseâ (6:31). Lukeâs report of Jesusâ words here resembles Matthewâs record of this saying, also found in an extended sermon (see Matt. 7:12). Likely given on different occasions, as are many duplicate teachings (see the Analysis on 6:20â49),19 the major thrust of Jesusâ two pronouncements encourages us to do good to others just as we want them to do good to us. He allows the positive incentive to rest within usânothing more, nothing less.

The classic formulation of doing good, of course, lies comfortably in the parable of the good Samaritan (see 10:25â37). As all know, Jesusâ narrative responds to a lawyerâs question laced with spite: âAnd who is my neighbour?â he hisses (10:29). Rather than lashing out at the questioner, Jesus tells a story of reprehensible neglect that contrasts sharply with genuine pity and compassionate help. Jesus makes his point razor sharp to his hearers, and particularly to the huffy lawyer, by placing a despised, distrusted Samaritan at the center of his parable. As many observe, here Jesus emphasizes in a vivid story the act of reaching out to others, no matter their origin or circumstance. When, at its end, Jesus asks the questioner who âwas neighbor unto him that fell among the thieves,â the man refuses to mouth the word âSamaritanâ and says simply, âHe that shewed mercy on him.â Jesusâ answer? âGo, and do thou likewiseâ (10:36â37; see the Analysis on 10:25â37).

D. Discipleship

Closely tied to doing good is the Saviorâs fundamental teaching of discipleship, of giving ourselves fully to the Savior and his ongoing work, an act that forms the wellspring of doing good that carries into eternityâsuch disciples shall receive âin the world to come life everlastingâ (18:30). This teaching hovers like an elegant perfume over all the Gospel accounts, demonstrating that this principle forms a major thrust of Jesusâ expectations. And becoming a disciple requires no small commitment. On one level, it involves bundling our family relationships inside our relationship with the Lord, trusting that he will enrich and make those relationships eternal (see the Notes on 6:48; 8:21; 14:26; 18:29â30; the Analysis on 11:14â28).

In his first extended treatment of discipleship (see 9:23â26), Jesus pairs his teaching with his first prophecy about his suffering, death, and resurrection (see 9:22). Plainly, discipleship derives its ultimate meaning from his Atonement. As Jesus surrenders his will to the Father, so we must be eagerly ready to surrender our wills to the Savior. Later he adds emphasis to this combinationâAtonement and discipleshipâby pairing them again.
