After his encounter with the rich young ruler that results in this manâs sorrowful withdrawal (see 18:18â25), Jesus responds to the question, âWho then can be saved?â by answering, âThere is no man that hath left house, or parents, or brethren, or wife, or children, for the kingdom of Godâs sake, Who shall not receive manifold more in this present time, and in the world to come life everlastingâ (18:26, 29â30). Discipleship, therefore, requires us to reorder what means most to us. Jesus points directly to our most enduring relationships, those within a family, and asks that we subsume them to our relationship with him (see the Notes on 6:48; 8:21; 14:26; 18:29â30; the Analysis on 14:25â35; 18:26â30).

Immediately thereafter, Jesus says to the Twelve, âwe go up to Jerusalemâ (18:31). And what will happen there? âall things that are written by the prophets concerning the Son of man shall be accomplished. For he shall be delivered unto the Gentiles, and shall be mocked, and spitefully entreated, and spitted on: And they shall scourge him, and put him to death: and the third day he shall rise againâ (18:31â33). In this light, it becomes evident that Jesus associates his demanding but rewarding discipleship with his Atonement that will occur at Jerusalem.

How much more demanding is his concept of discipleship? Besides touching our family relationships, it also affects our attachment to property and even our natural desire to keep living. Concerning property or the goods of this world, Jesus declares a person âthat forsaketh not all that he hath, he cannot be my discipleâ (14:33). Said another way, âwhat is a man advantaged, if he gain the whole world, and lose himself, or be cast away [from God]â (9:25), or, as Matthew records, âwhat is a man profited, if he shall gain the whole world, and lose his own soul?â (Matt. 16:26). Obviously, our interests in material wealth, whatever they may be, are not to steal our loyalty to the Savior (see the Note on 9:25).

On the question of strong loyalty to him, Jesus adds a third dimension to those of family and propertyâour lives. If we bend our energies to preserve our lives as they are, Jesus says that we âshall lose [them].â But âwhosoever will lose his life for my sake, the same shall save itâ (9:24). To illustrate how deeply serious he is about us spending our lives for him and his work, he teaches that we must âtake up [our] cross daily, and follow [him]â (9:23). He pushes this point later when he declares that the person who does ânot bear his cross, and come after me, cannot be my discipleâ (14:27). His pointer to the cross, an instrument of execution, speaks volumes. Like a condemned prisoner, we lift our discipleship onto our shoulders and begin a journey to a fixed place. But in contrast to a condemned person, our path leads in a direction that we ourselves determine, with our own fixed resolve, taking us to a place where we find the Savior (see the Notes on 9:23â24; 14:27; the Analysis on 9:23â27; 21:1â4).

E. Who Is Jesus?

In Lukeâs hands, Jesus stands a person beyond the ability of language to describe. We must therefore resist any temptation to approach him in a long, drawn-out discussion. Instead, we should proceed circumspectly and respectfully, relying on Lukeâs language and shrinking any discussion to the bare essentials. But Lukeâs language hovers at the core of the question. Does he set Jesus before us realistically and accurately, or does the real Jesus fade from our view, hidden in part by the way Luke and his sources present him? For Latter-day Saints, elements of the answer lie in Joseph Smithâs renaming Matthew and John as âtestimonies.â20 They are thus as individual as personal testimonies; they each approach Jesus from a different angle, even and especially when they center on the same experiences, in this case the saving ministry of the Savior. In this light, Lukeâs Gospel also stands as a testimony.

Almost all Christian commentators bracket Jesus, calling him the Lucan Jesus, as if he languishes remote and concealed behind layers of tradition and behind Lukeâs theological agenda. But commentators who have no stake in the question about how the real Jesus appears in Lukeâs Gospel see matters very differently. Two examples will suffice. First, when writing about the frightful night of Jesusâ arrest and nighttime hearing, the Jewish scholar David Flusser holds that âthe sequence of events during that awful night given in Luke makes sense, while in Mark (and Matthew) the description is at the very least strange and confused.â21 Such an assessment does not come lightly and underlines the value of Lukeâs record. Moreover, when examining the soldiersâ role in punishing Jesus when in Pilateâs custody (see 22:63â64), Flusser writes that âwe have found further evidence for the great value of the Gospel of Lukeâ in his accurate recounting of the cruel game played on Jesus after his arrest.22 In agreement with these judgments, the Christian scholar Richard Bauckham sees clear evidence that Luke relies on eyewitness testimony that makes his account a reliable story of the mortal Jesus. This judgment stands in contrast to the claim that early Christians valued the story of Jesus only in the context of the here and now of the churchâs existence, that is, that for early church members the story of Jesus possesses no real value for its own sake.23

Where does Luke get his eyewitness testimony that he claims to possess (see 1:1â4)? We begin with his access to Mary, Jesusâ mother, the most important witnesses to her sonâs life and ministry. Beside the tradition that puts her in Ephesus with the Apostle John, another tradition locates her in Jerusalem, where she lives out her life.24 Two shrines stand in memory of her burial there, on opposite sides of the Old City, the Dormition Abbey and the Virginâs Tomb. If, in fact, she is still in Jerusalem some twenty-three or twenty-four years after the death and resurrection of Jesus, a reasonable assumption, Luke surely meets her on his trip to Jerusalem with the Apostle Paul when they travel together. What is the evidence?

In three extended passages in the book of Acts, Luke adopts the pronoun âweâ when describing events (see Acts 16:10â17; 20:5â21:18; 27:1â28:16). It is clear that on these occasions he is a companion of Paul.25 At the end of the second âweâ section, we find Paul in Jerusalem almost seven days before his arrest by Roman soldiers (see Acts 21:27). Hence, Luke is in the capital city with him. Two nights later, soldiers escort Paul to Caesarea on the Mediterranean coast (see Acts 23:23â33). It is reasonable that this period of time, and likely a few days more (Luke does not accompany Paul to the seacoast with the military guard, of course), afford Luke an open opportunity to interview church members in Jerusalem about what they know of Jesusâ ministry, death, and resurrection, including his post-resurrection appearances.26 If by chance Mary is in town, she is one person whom Luke will want to meet. If she is not in the city in those days, he will seek out people who know her and her son. Thus, it is a simple step to postulate an occasion when Luke meets Mary or other relatives and acquaintances. Furthermore, as Paul does, so Luke surely consults with members of the Twelve about the Saviorâs life and ministry (see 1 Cor. 15:3â7; Gal. 1:18â19). And no reason exists to think that these people distort their memories of Jesus in the retelling.27

As a further example, we consider the four daughters of âPhilip the evangelistâ (Acts 21:8â9). These young women grow up in Jerusalem because we first hear of their father as a priesthood leader serving among the Greek-speaking members of the Church there (Acts 6:5). Hence, they are witnesses not only to the charitable ministrations of their father but also to the early work of the Twelve after the Saviorâs ascension. In fact, the older daughters may have met Jesus and then seen him after his resurrection, although this is uncertain. What is certain is that they meet prominent church members in Jerusalem during their youth and hear their stories, including possibly those of Jesusâ mother and his brothers (Acts 1:14). Thus, these young women become a credible source for Luke as he gathers information for his Gospel and book of Acts both during the âmany daysâ that he stays in their home (Acts 21:10) and during the two years that he resides in Caesarea while the Apostle Paul is under house arrest (Acts 24:27). They will be among the important witnesses whom Luke interviews, making their recollections a fundamental part of his written reports.28

What may be more intriguing is the question whether Luke spends the two years between Paulâs arrest and his trip to Rome, recounted in the third of the âweâ passages (see Acts 27:1â28:16), roaming around Palestine and gathering information.29 As I suggest in section V.C below, it appears that he at least visits Galilee.

From Cleopas, one of the two disciples walking to Emmaus, Luke captures the most important title that the Savior repeats for himself after the resurrection. When the Savior falls in with the two travelers, he calls himself âthe Christ,â that is, âthe Messiahâ or âthe Anointed One,â an action that establishes how he sees himself, now attested by witnesses (see the Note on 24:26).30 In an effort to emphasize this, Luke quotes the Risen Jesus repeating this title later in the presence of his beloved followers who gather together the evening after his resurrection (see the Note on 24:46; the Analysis on 24:13â35). Naturally, others label him âthe Christâ during his mortal ministry, including angels and demons. But the Savior plainly reserves the right to call himself by this title after he rises from the dead (see 2:11, 26; 4:41; 9:20; for common views of the Messiah, see 23:37; the Notes on 19:38; 20:41â44; the Analysis on 20:41â44).

The two angels who appear at the empty tomb introduce another title that, at that moment, bears equal weight with âthe Christâ as a post-resurrection appellation for the Risen Jesus. Their words on that occasion literally say in Greek, âWhy seek ye the Living One with the dead?â (24:5). The title translated âthe Living One,â of course, points directly at Jesus as the now eternally living God.31 He is both resurrected from the dead and the possessor of everlasting life in himself, life that he can offer to others (see the Note on 24:5; John 1:4; 6:57; 1 John 1:2).32

Close behind âthe Christâ and âthe Living Oneâ are titles that Jesus calls himself during his mortal life, titles that eyewitnesses hear from his lips. One of the most prominent is âthe Coming One,â a title that John the Baptist first repeats and others copy (see 3:16; 7:19; the Note on 3:16). More than this, Jesus repeats this appellation for himself in the presence of others, specifically his closest disciples, assuring its genuineness (see 13:35; the Notes on 19:38; 20:16; the Analysis on 19:28â40; 22:39â46). This âComing Oneâ bears the responsibility of both calling sinners to repentance and kindling a fire on earth, as well as carrying powers of purification and judgment (see 5:32; 12:49, 51â53).33

According to Luke, a second title that the mortal Jesus applies to himself is âthe Son of man.â Although this appellative generates a lot of debate among scholars whether Jesus really adopts this title for himself (see the Notes on 5:24; 9:22), modern scripture settles the matter, if this name carries through the centuries: âin the language of Adam, Man of Holiness is [Godâs] name, and the name of his Only Begotten is the Son of Man, even Jesus Christâ (Moses 6:57; see also Moses 7:35; the Analysis on 9:18â22).34 Under the title âSon of man,â for example, Jesus forgives sins and heals (see 5:24), is in charge of Sabbath (see 6:5), seeks out and saves the lost (see 19:10), and suffers, dies and rises from the dead (see 9:22, 44; 17:25; 18:31â33). Moreover, he will return suddenly as the Son of Man, bringing judgment to the earth (see 17:24â36).

One of the few titles that Jesus applies to himself is âmasterâ or âteacherâ (Greek didaskalos).35 It turns up in his instructions to Peter and John about preparing for the Passover meal that he will share with the Twelve. When they find the home where the meal is to be served, they are to say to the owner, âThe Master saith unto thee, Where is the guestchamber, where I shall eat the passover with my disciples?â (22:11). Here Jesus repeats a title by which the homeowner knows him, thereby acknowledging this title to be completely appropriate as it applies to himself. It is also clear that this unnamed man is a disciple who regularly calls Jesus by this honorific term (see 8:49; 9:38; 10:25; 18:18; etc.; the Notes on 7:40; 9:33; 22:11; the Analysis on 22:7â13).36

One further example is âLordâ (Greek kyrios). In Lukeâs Gospel, this term carries meanings as diverse as âsirâ and âJehovah.â In most instances, the pointer is to the God of the Israelites, thus acknowledging Jesusâ majesty in this office.37 And Luke is responsible for writing a lot of them (see 7:13, 31; 10:1; 11:39; 12:42; 13:15; etc.). Only once does Jesus apply this title to himself when he declares, âthe Son of man is Lord also of the sabbathâ (6:5). Especially here, when addressing the issue of how to observe the Sabbath, is it appropriate for Jesus to push himself forward as Lord. Why? Because the Sabbath represents âa sign between [the Lord] and [the Israelites] throughout your generations.â Further, the observance of this day forms a âperpetual covenantâ between God and his people (Ex. 31:13â17). Hence, the Sabbath offers the one regular opportunity for Godâs people to show their devotion to their Lord. Jesus now steps forward as that person, the same Lord who sets out the law of the Sabbath (see the Notes on 2:11; 4:16; 5:8; 7:13, 31).38

To complete this part of our review, we turn to the virtue that flows from Jesus, a virtue that overcomes illness and pushes aside the strictures of ritual uncleanness. A single Greek term, dynamis, is regularly rendered either âvirtueâ or âpower,â making them synonyms. This said, Luke preserves two reports that feature Jesus coming into direct contact with individuals who are ritually unclean. But there must have been other instances because he often walks among multitudes of people with various ailments, though Luke writes only two notices (see 4:40; 6:19). In the first report of an unclean individual, âa man full of leprosyâ approaches him, begging Jesus to âmake me clean.â In typical fashion, Jesus âput forth his hand, and touched him, saying, I will: be thou cleanâ (5:12â13). By touching this man, he surely contracts ritual uncleanness. Why? Because, according to the Mosaic law, any long-term skin diseaseâleprosy or otherwiseâbrings on the sufferer a state of uncleanness (see Lev. 13:1â46; the Note on 17:12), and a person who touches such an afflicted individual contracts uncleanness as well (see Num. 5:2â4).39 In the second report, an unnamed woman, with âan issue of blood twelve years,â approaches Jesus in a crowd and touches âthe border of his garment: and immediately her issue of blood stanchedâ (8:43â44). Knowing that she is ritually unclean, we might assume that Jesus is now rendered unclean. But that is not the case. Instead, his âvirtue is gone out of [him]â and âshe was healed immediatelyâ (8:46â47). As these two cases showâthe leper and the afflicted womanâthe power of Jesus overwhelms the uncleanness that they each bear in their bodies and makes them whole, leaving him unaffected (see the Notes on 5:13; 7:6; 8:47; 10:7; the Analysis on 5:12â16).

F. Jesus in His World

We ask, What can we learn from Luke about Jesusâ youth and adult years, and his place in his world? As a child, of course, Jesus learns to ride a donkey without a saddle or bridle, guiding the donkey with a long stick pressed to the side of its head, as Middle Eastern children still do. That skill does not abandon him when he sits on the young donkey and rides, without incident, down the steep western slope of the Mount of Olives and up into the city (see 19:35, 45). Incidentally, the fact that the donkey is youngâcalled âa coltââtells us that Jesus is not so big a man that the donkey cannot walk steadily with him on its back (see the Note on 19:30).

Still as a child, Jesus comes into contact with at least four spoken languages, and he presumably learns them all, at least to the degree that a child interacts with others in his or her world. The first language that he learns is Aramaic, the spoken tongue of his parents. It is the dominant language of ancient Palestine,40 although dialectical variations occur from place to place as the speech peculiarity of Galilee, attributed to Peter, illustrates (see the Note on 22:59). Because he is taken to Egypt at a young age, and evidently is there for some time, as Coptic tradition holds, he learns spoken Egyptian by playing with other children (see the Note on 2:39).41 When the family finally returns to Nazareth, the young Jesus will go to Hebrew school and learn to read the text of the Bible, grasping its subtleties as shown in the scene at the temple âin the midst of the doctorsâ who are both âhearing him, and asking him questionsâ (JST 2:46).

Further proof that he knows the Hebrew Bible lies in the story of his visit to the Nazareth synagogue (see 4:16â20). Although Hebrew is likely not spoken in the town, he learns it well enough to read the Bible aloud in synagogue services. Jesusâ next language acquisition is Greek, probably learned in Sepphoris, a nearby town desperately in need of skilled artisans to rebuild it after its destruction in 4 BC. Because of Josephâs skills, he is in demand. And when Jesus acquires basic building skills from his father, as is customary (see Matt. 13:55; Mark 6:3), Joseph doubtless takes him to work where he meets Greek-speaking foremen and co-workers (see the Note on 4:16; JST Matt. 3:25, âhe served under his fatherâ). Thereafter, Jesus demonstrates his ability with Greek in his interview with Pilate, an educated Roman who knows Greek but does not know Aramaic. According to all the sources, no interpreter stands between them in their conversation. Our conclusion is that Jesus knows and speaks the Greek language (see the Notes on 23:2â3).

From all indications, Jesus grows up in a normal home, complete with devotion to God and respect for law, most notably the Mosaic law. While he is an infant, his parents conform to the requirements both of purifying his new mother at the temple and of paying the five shekels for his redemption (see the Notes on 2:22â24; the Analysis on 2:21â24). As he grows up, of course, he participates in the customary worship practices of his society as illustrated not only by his appearance at the temple with his parents (see 2:41â51) but also by regular attendance at synagogue services (see the Note on 4:16). Moreover, as a youth he âwas subject unto [his parents]â (2:51) and, when learning a trade, âhe served under his fatherâ (JST Matt. 3:25). To be sure, because of his extraordinary gifts, his mother especially pays close attention to his youthful progress (see 2:19, 51). But we find nothing in the Gospels to indicate an unusual upbringing (see the Notes on 2:51 and 8:21), although Johnâs Gospel hints that his mother is already a witness to his remarkable powers (see John 2:1â5; JST John 2:1â5).

Early on, Luke signals Jesusâ practice of always attending worship services: âas his custom was, he went into the synagogue on the sabbath dayâ (4:16). Luke thus makes plain that this is Jesusâ habitual pattern so that we picture him in a synagogue service each Sabbath. As Jesus gathers disciples around himself, he will model for them how he wants them to worship after establishing his church. More than this, he respects the way that services are run in houses of worship as his act of sitting down to comment on scripture, a custom in his day, demonstrates (see the Note on 4:20).

Luke offers to us the sense that Jesus arises early, as do most of his contemporaries of course, and arrives at places of worship early. The illustration arises in Lukeâs description of Jesus teaching in the temple during the last week of his life. In a summarizing statement, he writes that in those few days âall the people came early in the morning to him in the temple, for to hear himâ (21:38; also John 8:2, âearly in the morning he came again into the templeâ). Besides mirroring the habits of his audience, Jesus also seems to be anxious to reach as many people as possible during these last days on earth (see the Note on 20:16; the Analysis on 20:9â16 and 23:27â33). Hence, he comes early to begin his day of teaching (see the Note on 21:38).

Several accounts tell us about a characteristic of Jesus that his acquaintances know: he is a vigorous, even dominant speaker. At every turn, it seems, he takes opportunity to teach, regularly controlling the conversation so that others have to stop and listen. Two examples will suffice. In a scene wherein he is the guest of honor for a meal in the home of Simon the Pharisee, he is approached by âa woman . . . which was a sinnerâ (7:37) who, as he reclines, proceeds to wash and anoint his feet (see 7:38). To the hostâs unspoken criticism about allowing this woman to touch him, Jesus responds by taking over the conversation and teaching all in the house about forgiveness. He simply dominates the scene (see the Note on 7:40). Later, at a dinner hosted by âone of the chief Pharisees,â Jesus senses the ill will of the host and the other guests when a man afflicted with âthe dropsyâ approaches him to be healed. The occasion is âthe sabbath dayâ (14:1â2). After healing the man, he once again takes over the conversation, first making a point about meeting basic needs on the Sabbath and then teaching about humility and a proper response to the poor (see 14:3â24; the Analysis on 14:1â6). In sum, in both scenes, Jesus purposely dominates the table talk to make his teachings heard (see 6:6â10; 7:36â50; 11:37â52; 13:10â17; the Note on 7:40; the Analysis on 5:27â32 and 14:1â6).

Closely tied to this aspect of Jesusâ personality is a dimension that penetrates and weaves itself through all the Gospel accounts. It is the sense that Jesus always has at hand a ready response, a stirring story, an illuminating illustration. It is as if he prepares for, almost uncannily, situations, queries, and challenges. For example, to the huffy lawyer who demands to know, âwho is my neighbour?â Jesus recites the timeless parable of the good Samaritan (see 10:25â37). To a guest in the home of a chief Pharisee who counters Jesusâ requirement that we reach out to âthe poor, the maimed, the lame, the blindâ (14:13) he repeats the enduring story of âthe great supperâ (see 14:16â24). To those who complain that Jesus eats with publicans and sinners he offers immediately the parables of the lost sheep, the lost coin and the prodigal son (see 15:1â32). And on it goes.

Luke and his fellow Synoptists, Mark and Matthew, portray the length of Jesusâ ministry to be much shorter than does John. According to the three synoptic Gospels, Jesus visits Jerusalem once as an adult for the Passover celebration that marks his death (see 19:45; Matt. 21:10; Mark 11:11). John, on the other hand, follows Jesus to Jerusalem twice for Passover feasts, and notes a third occasion, thus imparting the impression that his ministry lasts about three years (see John 2:13; 6:4; 11:55).42 Intriguingly, a little digging in Luke also leads to the conclusion that Jesus visits the capital city on multiple occasions and therefore his ministry runs for a longer period of time.43

The first hint lies in the story of Jesus as the guest of Martha and Mary in Marthaâs âhouseâ (10:38). We know from Johnâs Gospel that Martha and Mary reside in Bethany, an hourâs walk from Jerusalem (see John 11:1, 18; JST John 11:2, 17). In Lukeâs treatment, Jesus comes to Marthaâs home long before he arrives in the capital city. It is probable that Luke moves the story from its original setting in Bethany to a place in his narrative where it makes an important point about prayers and homes, topics that will arise immediately in chapter 11 (see the Analysis on 10:38â42; 11:1â4; 11:14â28). If, in fact, this report is out of place and belongs to an earlier visit by Jesus to Jerusalem, as is likely, then we possess a modest pointer to more than one trip to the capital city (see the Notes on 10:38; 13:34; 19:47; 22:9; 23:5, 28, 50; 24:13).

This initial impression finds reinforcement elsewhereâthe aggregate of several clues forms a proof of multiple visits. The parable of the good Samaritan that Jesus tells just before his visit to the home of Martha and Mary fits more naturally in the Jerusalem area than in a Galilean or Perean setting. For Jesus draws attention to the Jerusalem-Jericho road as the setting for his story (see 10:30). To be sure, a northern audience will grasp his point and many will be familiar with the road. But it makes more sense that he interacts with the lawyer in the neighborhood of that road, perhaps where they can both see it (see the Note on 10:30).

The two disciples traveling to Emmaus are not just walking to this town that lies a few miles west of Jerusalem; they most likely reside there (see the Note on 24:13). Their request that Jesus, unknown to them for the moment, share a meal with them points to their home, not to an inn (see the Note on 24:29). In addition, when we meet Joseph of Arimathea, originally from a town near Jerusalem, he is already sympathetic to Jesusâ cause if not a disciple (see 23:50â53). The question is, How do people who live in the south of the country, in or near the capital city, become disciples of Jesus, whose main activities take place in Galilee, in the north? The most natural explanation is that Jesus comes to Jerusalem on at least one occasion long before his last Passover and draws them to himself (see the Notes on 23:50â51).

Likewise, when Jesus instructs Peter and John to see that preparations are complete for the Last Supper, their question âWhere wilt thou that we prepare?â assumes that these Galilean fishermen know the city, including the upscale west side, hinting that they have been guests in those homes before (see the Note on 22:9). Further, the impression jumps out that the authorities in Jerusalem come to a decision about destroying Jesus very soon after he arrives in town, indicating that they know him and his reputation from earlier visits (see the Note on 19:47). This agrees with the charge that they level against him in front of Pilate, that âHe stirreth up the people, . . . beginning from Galilee to this placeâ (see the Note on 23:5). It is an easy conclusion to hold that Jesus visits Jerusalem more than once during his ministry (see the Analysis on 22:7â13).

G. The Gap

One of the most puzzling omissions in the four Gospels has to do with Jesusâ ministry to the departed spirits of the dead. But hints lie all across the New Testament. As Albrecht Oepke writes, âThat a journey to the lowest regions [by Jesus] preceded that to the upper is seldom emphasized in the NT but everywhere presupposedâ44 (see Matt. 12:40; Rom. 10:7; Eph. 4:9; 1 Pet. 3:18â20; 4:6; Rev. 1:18). In Lukeâs account, the first hint arises when Zacharias prophesies that the dawning messianic age, to be heralded by his infant son, will bring light not only to inhabitants of this world but also âgive light to them that sit in darkness and in the shadow of deathâ (1:79). These words rest on Isaiah 9:2, a passage that early Christians see as announcing Jesusâ descent into the spirit world to release the imprisoned souls (see the Note on 1:79).45

A second tip occurs when Jesus stands up in the Nazareth synagogue and reads the lines: âThe Spirit of the Lord . . . hath sent me . . . to preach deliverance to the captives, and . . . to set at liberty them that are bruisedâ (4:18; see the Note thereon). Centuries later, these words inspire Joseph F. Smith to write, when recalling his vision of the spirit world, that the Savior comes to the spirit prison, âdeclaring liberty to the captives who had been faithfulâ (D&C 138:18). Later, he notices that Jesus organizes his âchosen messengers . . . to . . . proclaim liberty to the captives who were bound, even to all who would repent of their sins and receive the gospelâ (D&C 138:31).46

A third clue lies in Jesusâ instructions to Simon Peter after giving a sermon out of his fishing boat. He commands, âLaunch out into the deep, and let down your nets for a draughtâ (5:4). Often, the term the deep or the depth (Greek bathos) in the Septuagint raises the specter of the sea, usually a place where the dead are to be found. But in a remarkable passage, Isaiah writes that the deep is to become a path of rescue, âa way of passage for the delivered and redeemedâ (LXX Isa. 51:10). The Apostle Paul mirrors this notion, noting that Jesusâ post-mortal work will have something to do with âthe deepâ (Rom. 10:7). Further, Ezekiel writes that those in need of redemption, as personified by Pharaoh and his dead warriors, reside in the depth (see LXX Ezek. 26:19â20; 31:14â15, 18; 32:18â21, 24). Hence, Jesusâ reference to âthe deepâ draws up both the doctrine of redemption and his role as redeemer, not only for those in this world but also for those in the world to come (see the Note on 5:4).

A fourth instance stands in Jesusâ warning words about evil influences on families (see 11:17â26). He describes âa strong manâ whose âgoods are in peace,â and then âa stronger than heâ who pushes him out of âhis palaceâ and takes âfrom him all his armourâ (11:21â22). In this scene, the âstrong manâ is the devil and the âstrongerâ is the Savior. Importantly, the âgoodsâ of the âstrong manâ are the captive souls whom Jesus rescues, whether in this life or the next (see the Notes on 11:21â22; the Analysis on 11:14â28).47

A fifth hint sits in an unexpected place. After the Risen Jesus joins the two disciples walking to the town of Emmaus, they rehearse to him events of recent days which lead to the Saviorâs crucifixion. Then, according to the King James Translation, they say, âto day is the third day since these things were doneâ (24:21). This translation understands the Greek verb agÅ in its impersonal sense, standing for the verb âto be.â But the personal understanding is to be preferred: âhe [Jesus] is spending the third dayâ (emphasis added).48 In these words we find a marker for Jesusâ activities following his death even though the disciples appear to misunderstand how these are to occur. It therefore seems evident that these disciples know his prior teachings on this subject which include his visit among the departed spirits (see the Notes on 23:43 and 24:21; see also D&C 138:11â37).

H. Gentiles

Embedded in the fabric of Lukeâs Gospel is an unabashed interest in Gentiles, what we might call universalism. One obvious reason is found in Lukeâs book of Acts wherein he will chronicle the growth of the church among Gentiles. What he finds in the story of the Savior are prior pointers to that later era when the gospel message will reach out to and embrace these people. Given Lukeâs interest, it is puzzling that he omits a story found in Markâs record that narrates Jesusâ trip to the Phoenician cities of Tyre and Sidon, gentile towns north of Galilee, and his help for a gentile woman, a Syrophenician by birth, who seeks his healing powers for her afflicted daughter (see Mark 7:24â30). But this story stands in a section of Mark that finds no similarities in Lukeâs report. Luke does not seem to know anything from this long portion, Mark 6:45â8:26, the so-called âgreat omission.â49 This omission, incidentally, raises questions about how much Luke follows Markâs account, an issue that we will come to later.

The first song sung for the Gentiles is voiced by the aged Simeon when he takes the infant Jesus in his arms at the Jerusalem temple and, in worshipful reverence, hymns the words, âmine eyes have seen thy salvation, which thou has prepared before the face of all people; a light to lighten the Gentilesâ (2:30â31). Thus, with prophetic foresight, Simeon sees the long-range impact of Jesusâ ministry, as Luke will begin to rehearse in the book of Acts. But he also foresees closer events that will arise during Jesusâ mortal life. Examples follow.

Initially, Jesus himself signals his deep interest in Gentiles in the Nazareth synagogue, among friends and neighbors. After he reads a scripture passage from Isaiah, he sits down to offer comment, a customary act. In his oral commentary, he brings up two stories from the Old Testament that illustrate Godâs compassion for Gentiles: Elijah and the feeding of the widow of Sarepta and her son during an extended famine as well as the healing of Naaman, the Syrian leper, by Elisha (see 4:25â27; the Analysis on 4:16â30; 1 Kgs. 17:8â16; 2 Kgs. 5:1â14). The hateful response of the congregation to his references to these stories demonstrates that he faces prejudice in its worst forms in his society (see 4:28â29).

A miraculous case arises in Capernaum when a centurion, a Roman military officer, seeks Jesusâ help in healing a âdearâ servant now near death (see 7:1â10). Jesus responds immediately by walking to the gentile manâs home, ready to step onto his property, an act that will render Jesus unclean in the eyes of fellow Jews. Although he does not go into the manâs home, his willingness to do so demonstrates his caring concern (see the Notes on 7:2, 6â7; the Analysis on 7:1â10).

We soon run into another instance when Jesus and his disciples sail to the east shore of the Sea of Galilee, the gentile side, and encounter a bedeviled man who dwells âin the tombsâ (8:26â39). Even though Jesus casts out the devils who reside in the man, the local residents beg him to leave because he allows the devils to go into their swine and to cause them to stampede into the lake. Jesus departs, apparently defeated in his first attempt to bring his message to Gentiles, but instructs the man to return âto thine own house, and shew how great things God hath done unto thee.â The result is the man never stops talking. He recounts âthroughout the whole city how great things Jesus had done unto himâ (8:39). Unknowingly, he prepares his people for members of the Seventy who show up a few months later, with Jewish eating laws suspended so that they can accept the hospitality of Gentiles (see 10:7â8). Do they enjoy a harvest among Gentiles? Their response after their mission indicates that they do (see 10:17). Hence, what appears to be a failure on Jesusâ part to penetrate a gentile population on the east side of the lake turns into a bountiful harvest of souls (see the Notes on 8:26, 39; 10:7â8; the Analysis on 8:26â40 10:1â12).

Certainly the biggest indicator of Jesusâ interest in Gentiles emerges in his call of the Seventy. The number itself points to the gentile nations, matching the number of names in the table of gentile nations (see Gen. 10).50 To facilitate the Seventyâs mission, Jesus suspends kosher laws so that they can go among Gentiles: when they enter a gentile home, they are to eat and drink âsuch things as they giveâ and] âare set before [them]â (10:7â8). As modern scripture affirms, the Seventy are âcalled to preach the gospel, and to be especial witnesses unto the Gentilesâ (D&C 107:25; see the Note on 10:1; the Analysis on 10:1â12). Hence, their mission signals Jesusâ intent to fold Gentiles into his kingdom.

Other passages add emphasis to this portrait. When Jesus wants to draw attention to his divine status, he chooses to cite the stories of the Queen of Shebaâs visit to Solomon and the Ninevitesâ repentance at the preaching of the prophet Jonas (11:31â32; see the Notes thereon; the Analysis on 11:29â32). Besides this, the Joseph Smith Translation offers a final example. At 12:30, Jesus speaks of âthe nations of the worldâ that seek for the necessities of life. At the end of this verse, the Prophet Joseph Smith adds an entire verse that, at its opening, highlights the disciplesâ future mission among Gentiles: âAnd ye are sent unto them [the Gentiles] to be their ministersâ (JST 12:33). Hence, long before these disciples go into the wide world carrying the message of their Savior, he points them precisely in that direction (see the Note on 12:30).

III. The Text

A. The Joseph Smith Translation

Latter-day Saints accept as inspired the changes that the Prophet Joseph Smith introduces into the text of the Bible, including titling the Gospels âTestimony.â He was under divine mandate to undertake an effort to go through the Bible and, under inspiration, to make changes and adjustments, producing âthe scriptures . . . as they are in [the Lordâs] own bosomâ (D&C 35:20).51 Latter-day Saints call this product the Joseph Smith Translation, though the term âtranslationâ does not bear the sense of a translation from one language to another. Instead, it has to do with a fresh rendering of the text, only a few of whose new readings are to be found in ancient copies of the Bible. Notably, the first addition to Lukeâs Gospel occurs in the opening verse of the King James Translation: âForasmuch as many have taken in handâ (1:1). Before this expression Joseph Smith inserts, âAs I am a messenger of Jesus Christ, and knowing that many have taken in handâ (JST 1:1; emphasis added). By this insertion, the Prophet Joseph emphasizes in clear fashion the value of Lukeâs record because it comes from the hand of âa messenger of Jesus Christ.â

Generally, the most important alterations in Luke either add to or change the words of Jesus, offering to us a brimming cornucopia of his teachings. Others fill out the background of an episode reported by Luke, some offer interpretation, and still others augment the written account in other ways.52 We discuss all the important changes in the commentary that follows this introduction. But a few examples are in order here.

One of the most significant additions to Jesusâ words occurs in his commentary following his meeting with the rich young ruler (see 18:18â30). In effect, after warning about riches throughout his ministry, Jesus throws open the gates of heaven to the wealthy. But with provisos. In the KJV Translation, Jesus warns, âHow hardly shall they that have riches enter into the kingdom of God!â (18:24). Even though a little later Jesus says, âThe things which are impossible with men are possible with Godâ (18:27), there is little here to comfort the person of means. Then, leaving the former saying untouched, Joseph Smith introduces a stunning few words to Jesusâ latter saying and changes the whole tone: âIt is impossible for them who trust in riches, to enter into the kingdom of God; but he who forsaketh the things which are of this world, it is possible with God, that he should enter inâ (JST 18:27). Like a fresh breeze, these words elevate the possibilities for the wealthy to become part of Godâs kingdom (see the Note on 18:27; the Analysis on 18:26â30).

In a second instance that speaks to our inner motives, especially when we are alone and unobserved by others, Joseph Smith adds crucial lines to the parable of the good Samaritan (see 10:25â37). As Luke records Jesusâ story, after the unnamed traveler is attacked and left for dead, âa certain priest . . . when he saw him, he passed by on the other side.â A Levite exhibits the same behavior and âpassed by on the other sideâ (10:31â32). In summarizing the neglect demonstrated by these two religious leaders, the Prophet Joseph adds seventeen remarkable words of Jesus that uncover the leadersâ inner motivations and amplify their negligence: âfor they desired in their hearts that it might not be known that they had seen himâ (JST 10:33; see the Notes on 10:31â32; the Analysis on 10:25â37). These additional words strike at our motivations for turning away from someone in need.

A third example deals with the well-known act of speaking evil about the Son of Man. Jesus first addresses the twin matters of confessing and denying him: âWhosoever shall confess me before men, him shall the Son of man also confess before the angels of God: But he that denieth me before men shall be denied before the angels of Godâ (12:8â9). At this juncture, the Joseph Smith Translation adds two verses that illumine the reason for Jesusâ treatment of this subjectâit has to do with the disciplesâ public actions that are undermining Jesusâ ministry. âNow his disciples knew that he said this, because they had spoken evil against him before the people; for they were afraid to confess him before men. And they reasoned among themselves, saying, He knoweth our hearts, and he speaketh to our condemnation, and we shall not be forgiven. But he answered them, and said unto them, Whosoever shall speak a word against the Son of man . . .â (JST 12:10â12; emphasis added; see also JST Mark 14:36â37). To continue, in this classic passage, Jesus declares, âwhosoever shall speak a word against the Son of man, it shall be forgiven himâ (12:10). To this, the Prophet Joseph Smith adds two clarifying words: âWhosoever shall speak a word against the Son of man, and repentethâ (JST 12:12; emphasis added). Does Jesus leave a door open for his disciples to escape guilt? Yes, through repentance.

Among other types of changes we find simple additions that form a bridge between narratives. A good example appears at 14:25. Before this verse, Jesus rehearses a story about a âgreat supperâ to a group gathered for a Sabbath meal (14:1, 16). After Lukeâs report of Jesusâ words, Luke abruptly takes Jesus outside the home and records that âthere went great multitudes with himâ (14:25). The Joseph Smith Translation adds ten words, smoothing Jesusâ movement from inside the home to his congregated disciples outside, and incidentally fills in a detail about Jesusâ Sabbath activity that day: âAnd when he had finished these sayings, he departed thenceâ and taught his followers about discipleship (JST 14:25â28; see the Notes on 14:25â27).

In another example, we glimpse the youthful Jesus in the temple while his parents are frantically looking for him. When they finally locate him âin the temple, [he is] sitting in the midst of the doctors, both hearing them, and asking them questionsâ (2:46). The Joseph Smith Translation turns this scene on its head. Accordingly, when Jesusâ parents find him âsitting in the midst of the doctors, . . . they were hearing him, and asking him questionsâ (JST 2:46). Jesus is not the learner but the teacher, even at a young age (see the Note on 2:46; the Analysis on 2:40â52).

We look at a final instance from the Gospel text, this one as Jesus hangs on the cross. All readers of the New Testament know the words that he utters while he is suspended between the two criminals: âFather, forgive them; for they know not what they doâ (23:34). Commentators agree that Jesus gasps out these words in a final act of mercy toward those who conspire to bring him to this end: âthe Jewish âleadersâ . . . those who were crucifying and mocking him.â53 But Joseph Smith adds an explanatory glossâin parenthesesâthat abruptly changes this sense: â(Meaning the soldiers who crucified him)â (JST 23:35). Clearly, according to the addition, Jesusâ forgiveness extends to the soldiers alone, not yet to the Jewish authorities who engineer his execution (see the Note on 23:34; the Analysis on 23:34â38).

We now turn to the matter of calling Lukeâs Gospel a âtestimony,â an issue noted in the first sentence of this section above. Not only does Joseph Smith retitle the New Testament Gospels as testimonies in the Joseph Smith Translation, a process begun in March 1831 (see D&C 45:60â62), but the Lord retitles them too in a revelation given on December 27, 1832, when he calls Johnâs Gospel âthe testimony of Johnâ (D&C 88:3, 141). The model for such titling, of course, appears in the last lines of the fourth Gospel when, reading about the totality of that Gospelâs record, we see it called âhis [Johnâs] testimonyâ (John 21:24; Greek martyria). This same Greek term appears in 1 John 5:9â11, translated variously in the KJV as âwitnessâ and ârecord,â and points to the entirety of Jesusâ life and ministry.54

When citing passages in the Joseph Smith Translation, I adopt the chapters and verses as published in The Holy Scriptures: Inspired Version.55 This volume is widely available to readers and its numbering system is adopted in the âJoseph Smith Translationâ excerpts in the back of the LDS edition of the Bible. I have not chosen the citation pattern adopted more recently in other compilations.56

B. Modern Scriptureâs Contribution

Above all hovers a sense that the basic story of Jesus in the New Testament Gospels is reliable and accurate, as several summarizing passages demonstrate (see 2 Ne. 6:9â11; 10:3â6; 25:12â14; Mosiah 13:33â35; Alma 7:10â12; 34:8â14). In one broad statement, the Resurrected Savior characterizes the Old and New Testaments as âthat which the prophets and apostles have writtenâ (D&C 52:9). In a moving, poetic passage from the Book of Mormon,57 with more detail, we witness the sweep of Jesusâ life and ministry, beginning in premortality:

For behold,the time cometh,and is not far distant,that with power,the Lord Omnipotentwho reigneth,who was,and isfrom all eternity to all eternity,shall come down from heaven among the children of men,and shall dwell in a tabernacle of clay,and shall go forth amongst men,working mighty miracles,such as healing the sick,raising the dead,causing the lame to walk,the blind to receive their sight,and the deaf to hear,and curing all manner of diseases.And he shall cast out devils,or the evil spirits which dwell in the hearts of the children of men.And lo, he shall suffer temptations,and pain of body,hunger,thirst,and fatigue,even more than man can suffer, except it be unto death;for behold, blood cometh from every pore,so great shall be his anguish forthe wickednessand the abominations of his people.And he shall be called Jesus Christ,the Son of God,the Father of heaven and earth,the Creator of all things from the beginning;and his mother shall be called Mary.And lo, he cometh unto his own,that salvation might come unto the children of meneven through faith on his name;and even after all thisthey shall consider him a man,and say that he hath a devil,and shall scourge him,and shall crucify him.And he shall rise the third day from the dead;and behold, he standeth to judge the world;and behold, all these things are donethat a righteous judgment might come upon the children of men. (Mosiah 3:5â10)

Besides this broad endorsement of the Gospelsâ accounts, we come upon passages in the Book of Mormon that focus on specific scenes from Jesusâ mortal experience (see Morm. 9:22â25). Concerning his suffering and death, we hear: âwhen the day cometh that the Only Begotten of the Father . . . shall manifest himself unto them in the flesh, behold, they will reject him. . . . Behold, they will crucify him; and after he is laid in a sepulchre for . . . three days he shall rise from the deadâ (2 Ne. 25:12â13). These words of the prophet Nephi, uttered 600 years before Jesusâ death, rest in part on written works not included in our Bibles: âthe God of our fathers . . . yieldeth himself, according to the words of the angel, . . . into the hands of wicked men, to be lifted up, according to the words of Zenock, and to be crucified, according to the words of Neum, and to be buried in a sepulchre, according to the words of Zenosâ (1 Ne. 19:10).

Matters do not end here. A number of Jesusâ teachings which appear in other Gospels find their way into his visit to the New World, at least as many as Mormon records. Two brief examples will illustrate. First, the complete text of the Sermon on the Mount, with a few significant changes, appears in 3 Nephi 12â14, underscoring the authenticity of Jesusâ sermon recorded in Matthew 5â7.58 Second, the Resurrected Jesus quotes lines from John 10:16: âother sheep I have which are not of this fold; them also I must bring, and they shall hear my voice; and there shall be one fold, and one shepherdâ (3 Ne. 15:17, 21). Of course, his quotation shows that this saying is authentic. But there is more. For he explains to his New World hearers that, when in Palestine, he says nothing further about this subject to his Old World followers âbecause of stiffneckedness and unbeliefâ (3 Ne. 15:18). Moreover, the Old World disciples do not grasp his meaning because âthey supposed it had been the Gentilesâ who are to hear his voice, not comprehending that the Savior âshould not manifest [himself] unto them save it were by the Holy Ghostâ (3 Ne. 15:22â23). Here we catch a glimpse into the relationship between the mortal Jesus and his disciples: he obviously does not explain everything to them unless they ask. And part of their lack of curiosity rests on a lack of belief.

On the other hand, a rich set of teachings to his disciplesâmany unrecorded in the Gospelsâsurfaces in the Doctrine and Covenants in revelations to the Prophet Joseph Smith. Here we learn that the Gospels do not present the whole story, whether because the Gospel writers do not know it or because they are abbreviating. We offer a few examples.

In a notable teaching, Jesus gives âthe lawâ to his âancient prophets and apostlesâ to ârenounce war and proclaim peaceâ (D&C 98:16, 32). As he frames its modern formulation, they are to avoid lashing out at those who persecute them and their families, and to withstand abuse âpatiently and revile not against them, neither seek revenge.â Remarkably, they are to weather any such mistreatment a second and a third time. Only after the fourth are they justified in initiating action against the enemy (D&C 98:23â38). The only teaching of Jesus reported in Lukeâs Gospel that distantly resembles this âlawâ rises to view in a discussion about forgiving: âIf thy brother trespass against thee, . . . and if he repent, forgive him. And if he trespass against thee seven times in a day, and seven times in a day . . . [says] I repent; thou shalt forgive himâ (see the Notes on 17:3â4; the Analysis on 17:1â4). The two teachings are not the same, but they breathe the same fragrance of forgiveness.

Concerning the intriguing terms âendless tormentâ and âeternal damnation,â the Lord says that, from a teaching episode while he is with them, âmine apostlesâ have known their meaning: âFor, behold, I am endless,â says the Savior, âand the punishment which is given from my hand is endless punishment, for Endless is my name.â In this light, âEternal punishment is Godâs punishmentâ and âEndless punishment is Godâs punishment,â for âit is not written that there shall be no end to this tormentâ (D&C 19:6â12). Nothing of the sort appears in the Gospel accounts.

According to modern scripture, when the Savior introduces his sermon on the Mount of Olives after coming to Jerusalem for the last time, he wants to show to his Apostles âhow the day of redemption shall come.â He then notes that the Twelve âhave looked upon the long absence of your spirits from your bodies to be a bondage,â a misconception that he will correct by setting out âhow the day of redemption shall comeâ (D&C 45:17). From this passage, it becomes evident that either Jesus knows their worries because of his divine ability to read their thoughts (see the Note on 5:22) or they share their fears with him in conversation. In either case, the New Testament Gospels preserve nothing from this concern of the Apostles.

In this connection, modern scripture offers a first-hand report of Jesusâ sermon on the Mount of Olives, one that he himself recalls (see D&C 45:16â59). In introducing this fresh rehearsal of the sermon, the Resurrected Lord says, âI stood before them [the disciples] in the flesh, and spake unto themâ (D&C 45:16). This detail, not preserved by Luke, differs from the other accounts which record that âhe sat upon the mount of Olivesâ when delivering the sermon (Matt. 24:3; Mark 13:3). In this light, modern scripture provides a modest but important correction, signaling other adjustments to follow. Furthermore, we learn why he chooses to give this sermon: âAs ye [the disciples] have asked of me concerning the signs of my coming, in the day when I shall come in my glory in the clouds of heaven, to fulfil the promises that I have made unto your fathersâ (D&C 45:16). Obviously, on prior occasions, not reported in the Gospels, his disciples have asked him about these seminal, future events. He now will answer them, adding details as he goes (see 21:5â36 and the Notes thereon).

When Peter, James, and John join Jesus on the Mount of Transfiguration, among other items that they witness, as narrated in modern scripture, they behold how âthe earth shall be transfiguredâ at the end-time, at âthe day of transfiguration.â This part of their experience does not appear in any of the Gospel narratives because âthe fulnessâ of that experience âye have not yet receivedâ (D&C 63:20â21). We discover, therefore, that the New Testament Gospels do not record the complete event, nor does any other ancient source (see 2 Pet. 1:16â18). This point underlines the distinct possibility of other important omissions, including Jesusâ other trips to Jerusalem,59 the salting of the large catch of fish, and Jesusâ extended stay with Zacchaeus (see the Notes on 5:6, 11; 9:22, 33; 10:30, 38; 13:34; 19:5, 47; 22:9; 23:5, 28, 50; 24:13). Only modern revelation alerts us to this fact (see the Analysis on 9:28â36).

In a passage that treats forgiveness, we learn from modern scripture that, sadly, Jesusâ âdisciples, in days of old, sought occasion against one another and forgave not one another in their heartsâ (D&C 64:8). Whether the Savior has in mind the disputation that arises during the Last Supper over âwhich of them should be accounted the greatestâ (22:24) or another occasion we cannot know. It may be that the Apostles are not implicated but others from the entourage that follows Jesus, or still others from a later era (see 1 Cor. 1:11; 3:3; 11:16â22). But the disagreement is serious enough that âfor this evil they were afflicted and sorely chastenedâ (D&C 64:8). Such an observation does not appear in the Gospel accounts.

In another vein, modern scripture fills out one particular promise of Jesus to his Apostles: âI appoint unto you a kingdom,â he says to them, wherein they will âeat and drink at my table . . . and sit on thronesâ (22:29â30; see the Notes thereon). It turns out that there are other, magnificent parts in this promise that have to do with Jesusâ Second Coming. From modern revelation we learn that these beloved, trusted men are the beneficiaries of âa firm decree,â issued by the Father, that âmine apostles, the Twelve which were with me [Jesus] in my ministry at Jerusalem, shall stand at my right hand at the day of my [second] coming.â Moreover, they will be standing âin a pillar of fire.â What is more, they will be âclothed with robes of righteousness, with crowns upon their heads,â bathed âin glory even as I amâ (D&C 29:12). Soon after this moment, they will fill their prophesied role as judges.

Notably, the same passage sets out specific dimensions of Jesusâ promise that the Apostles will judge âthe twelve tribes of Israelâ (22:30), a promise that also appears, prophetically, in the Book of Mormon more than six hundred years before they are called, a fact that underlines the importance of their function as judges (see 1 Ne. 12:9). Explicitly, while âclothed with robes of righteousness,â they will âjudge the whole house of Israel.â Modern scripture then defines who âthe whole house of Israelâ isâthese are they who âhave loved me [the Savior] and kept my commandments, and none elseâ (D&C 29:12). Thus, as scripture makes plain, the status as members of the âhouse of Israelâ is hardly tied to lineage. Everything has to do with following the Savior; everything has to do with discipleship.

A final two points from modern scripture confirm the picture from the New Testament Gospels. First, before departing from his disciples, the Risen Jesus gives a charge to them, with promises, about their future ministries (see 24:47â49; Matt. 28:19â20; Mark 16:15â18; Acts 1:6â8). Quoting the Resurrected Christ, the Book of Mormon essentially confirms this scene, quoting almost exactly Mark 16:15â18 (see Morm. 9:22â24). But the Doctrine and Covenants adds considerably to the words of the Risen Jesus as he departs from his disciples. For example, in words not recorded elsewhere, the Lord says to the Apostles, âevery soul who believeth on your words, and is baptized by water for the remission of sins, shall receive the Holy Ghost.â In the realm of miracles, he promises that âIn my name they [his disciples] shall open the eyes of the blind, and unstop the ears of the deaf; And the tongue of the dumb shall speak.â None of these miraculous manifestations receives mention in another source. Further, the Resurrected Savior repeats a caution that he gives to the Apostles: âthey shall not boast themselves of these things, neither speak them before the worldâ (D&C 84:64, 69â70, 73). This command to be quiet about these promises and instructions may be one reason that they appear nowhere in other scripture.

Second, according to Luke 24:49, the Risen Savior instructs the eleven to âtarry . . . in the city of Jerusalem, until ye be endued with power from on high.â They are not to leave, even if they have pressing business elsewhere. Instead, they are to make themselves available for his instruction concerning âthe things pertaining to the kingdom of God,â instruction that will occur during the forty-day period following his resurrection (Acts 1:3). Then they will âbe endued with power from on highâ at Pentecost (see Acts 2:1â42). This directive, though seemingly minor, is so important that the Lord draws attention to this former command when directing Joseph Smith to remain in Kirtland, Ohio, in 1833 (see D&C 95:9; the Note on 24:49).

C. Unity of the Gospel

Many commentators unbind Lukeâs Gospel and wrap it into two parts. The first consists of chapters 1 and 2; the second ties together chapters 3 through 24. This view arises largely because of the distinctive character of the first two chaptersâthey link to a different spirit from what we find in the later chapters because of the presence of âSemiticismsâ and exhibit a dependance on sources not relied on elsewhere in Lukeâs report. Thus, chapter 3, for some, forms the formal opening of the Gospel story with chapters 1 and 2 a later addition.60 Although positing such a separation may rest on careful analysis, threads of unity prove that the two parts stand together and are divided only artificially. The strongest threads consist of multiple occurrences of a characteristic known as inclusio, most often a distinctive term or phrase that appears only at the opening of a work and then near its end, signaling a bridging literary tie from beginning to end. Actually, the number of such expressions are too numerous to discuss. I count at least twenty-one such unifying links.61 Three examples will put a wrap on this literary device.

The pointer to the temple, specifically the sanctuary (Greek naos),62 frames one of the earliest examples of an inclusio. In Zachariasâ duties as a priest, he enters the sanctuary to kindle the incense, the only time that he will perform this act in his lifetime. Hence, Luke is drawing our attention to a special occasion that takes place only in the sanctuary (see 1:9, 21, 22; the Note on 1:9). The word naos appears again near the end of Lukeâs Gospel when he writes about another scene in the sanctuary, this one of an entirely different character: âthe veil of the temple [sanctuary] was rent in the midstâ (23:45). Thus, Luke takes us into the sanctuary, next to the veil, on two occasions and tells us of hopeful revelation in the first case and of Godâs unhappy hand in the second (see the Note on 23:45). As an added note, even though Luke draws attention to the temple in other passages throughout his Gospel, the Greek word is not the same (hieron) and refers to the whole temple complex (see 2:37, 46; 4:9; 19:45; etc.; the Note on 2:27).63

An unusual inclusio manifests itself in the actions of women, specifically actions that follow an encounter with angels. In the first, Mary goes off to see her distant cousin Elisabeth after the angel visits her. The angel does not tell her to go, but Mary clearly senses an imperative that she do so when the angel informs her that âthy cousin Elisabeth . . . hath also conceived a son in her old age: and this is the sixth month with herâ (1:36). In the second scene the women at the empty tomb sense an imperative in the words of the angels who tell them, âHe [Jesus] is not here, but is risenâ (24:6). In response, they âreturned from the sepulchre, and told all these things unto the eleven, and to all the restâ (24:9). Thus, they become first-rank eyewitnesses of the resurrection.64 Plainly, both Mary and the women disciples from Galilee act on a similar impulseâthey grasp that the words of the angels give them specific direction. And they respond accordingly (see the Notes on 1:39; 24:9).

The third example has to do with a verb. The passive form of the verb horaÅ, which is ÅphthÄ, meaning âwas seen,â65 occurs in three passages: at 1:11 when the angel is seen by Zacharias, at 22:43 when the angel is seen by Jesus in Gethsemane, and at 24:34 when the Risen Lord is seen by Peter. The King James translators render all three as âappeared,â emphasizing the sense that the celestial being does the appearing to the mortal person. But the verbâs passive form communicates this sense only lightly. Rather, the emphasis rests fully on the direct sensory perception of the mortal individual, that is, on Zacharias who sees the angel, on Jesus who sees the angel, and on Peter who sees the Resurrected Christ. Particularly in the reports of the Resurrected Savior coming to disciples, Luke skirts the language of visions or dreams and, instead, stresses the unmediated physical encounter, as these three passages illustrate (see the Notes on 1:11; 22:43; 24:34).66 This emphatic language appears only at the beginning of Lukeâs Gospel and near its end, framing a notable inclusio.

D. The Framework

Luke constructs the framework of his record from the major themes that he hammers together from the Saviorâs activities and teachings as well as, briefly, from John the Baptist. We take notice of the following broad outline.

1. Infancy narratives (1:1â2:52)

2. John the Baptist and the Genealogy of Jesus through his father (3:1â38)

4. Galilean Ministry (4:1â9:50)

5. Journey to Jerusalem (9:51â19:48)

6. From the Temple Cleansing to the Ascension (20:1â24:53)

Lukeâs reports of the infancy stories and the journey to Jerusalem shape the two most distinctive elements. Otherwise, he follows the widely accepted pattern to portray Jesusâ life and ministry, especially as Mark, the earliest written Gospel, frames it. In the infancy narrative, of course, Luke shares an interest with Matthew for rehearsing events that impact Jesusâ parents and then the child Jesus. But Luke steers a different course when highlighting those events. For instance, according to Matthew the earliest divine communication comes to Joseph after he finds himself in a quandary about Maryâs newly disclosed pregnancy (see Matt. 1:18â21). Luke, on the other hand, pushes forward the angelâs visits to Zacharias and Mary as the divine disclosures of Godâs intentions to bring about his long-awaited purposes through the infants John and Jesus. According to Matthew, the most notable visitors to the child Jesus and his parents are the âwise men from the eastâ (see Matt. 2:1â12). Lukeâs story emphasizes not prominence but humility in recounting the visit of shepherds to see the new-born Jesus (see 2:8â17). Matthewâs hands fashion no early connection between the parents of the two children whereas Luke writes about not only the family relationship between the mothers but especially Maryâs extended visit to Elisabeth (see 1:36, 39â56). Contrarily, Luke seems to know nothing of Jesusâ familyâs long stay in Egypt (see the Note on 2:39 and the Analysis on 2:36â39) whereas Matthew rehearses the flight of Joseph and Mary to Egypt that lasts until the angel announces to Joseph that âthey are dead which sought the young childâs lifeâ (Matt. 2:20).67 In this light, we conclude that Luke and Matthew draw from entirely different streams of information. And even though scholars accuse them of making up much of what they report, little compelling reason exists to believe that they create these infancy stories from whole cloth. Rather, they are faithful recorders of what comes to them.68

Lukeâs narrative of Jesusâ journey to Jerusalem is unique (see 9:51â19:48). According to Luke, Jesus breaks off his Galilean ministry and takes up an extended, meandering journey through towns and villages, with Jerusalem as his goal: âwhen the time was come that he should be received up, he stedfastly set his face to go to Jerusalemâ (9:51). As he goes from place to place, he âsent messengersâ to announce his arrival (9:52). After all, Jesus will pass through these towns only once and he seeks to maximize his contact with citizens (see 9:57; 10:38; 13:22; 14:25; 17:11; 18:31, 35; 19:1, 28; the Notes on 9:51â52). Hints exist in modern scripture that this sending forth of messengers becomes a pattern that Jesusâ Apostles will imitate as they establish the early church: âtake with you those who are ordained unto the lesser priesthood, and send them before you to make appointments, and to prepare the way. . . . Behold, this is the way that mine apostles, in ancient days, built up my church unto meâ (D&C 84:107â8). Not incidentally, such words point to the authenticity of what Luke describes when introducing Jesusâ journey to Jerusalem.

Lukeâs record of the journey refines and greatly expands the accounts that track Jesusâ travels into Perea on the east side of the Jordan River before he turns to Jerusalem for the last time (see Matt. 19:1â20:16; Mark 10:1â31).69 But he preserves only two incidents from this part of Jesusâ ministry, as Matthew and Mark report it, the blessing of children and the encounter with the rich young ruler (see 18:15â30; Matt. 19:13â30; Mark 10:13â27). Remarkably, within the journey account, Luke repeats a number of Jesusâ sayings and stories that we find in other contexts and in somewhat dissimilar language, as Matthew and Mark rehearse them. Naturally, the question arises whether Luke moves these items to a new yet artificial context or whether he is setting out instances wherein Jesus essentially repeats a parable or story to a completely different audience and, after adjusting it, makes a fresh point.

E. Repeated Sayings

For many commentators, similar sayings and stories in the Gospels are mere variants of one another. The only mystery to solve is to determine the earliest form of a particular saying or story as the Gospels preserve it. Form critics have tried their hand at this task and their efforts fail to convince much of their audience. An approach with promise holds to a view that Jesus utters similar words to separate audiences and adjusts his teachings to their needs.70 A few examples will illustrate.

Letâs start with the parable of the pounds (see 19:11â27) and a similar story, the parable of the talents (see Matt. 25:14â30). It is widely trumpeted that these parables simply represent different versions of one another that the Gospel writers, or early church members, adjust to suit their needs.71 But the variations between them are substantial, including the settings. In Matthew, Jesus utters the parable of the talents as a part of his discourse on the Mount of Olives. In Luke, he tells the parable of the pounds in or near Jericho in order to correct a crowdâs misplaced expectation that the kingdom of God will appear when Jesus sets foot in Jerusalem (see the Note on 19:11; the Analysis on 19:11â27). Further, the sheer number of differences in detail between the two parables, such as the main character in each, lead to the natural conclusion that Jesus tells similar stories that carry targeted messages to different audiences on different occasions.72

From the sayings on forgiving others, Matthew records that Jesus presses upon Peter the precept that he forgive another person up to âseventy times sevenâ (Matt. 18:22). According to Luke, Jesus makes a similar statement when teaching a general principle about those who trespass against us: âif [the trespasser] trespass against thee seven times in a day, and seven times in a day turn again to thee, saying, I repent; thou shalt forgive himâ (17:4). Here too, although the sayings share the number seven, the settings differ completely and Jesusâ words vary sharply from one telling to the other. We conclude, therefore, that Jesus utters the sayings on two different occasions (see the Analysis on 17:1â4).73

From Jesus, Luke records two sayings about lighting lamps and putting them on lampstands (see 8:16; 11:33; the Notes thereon). We find similar sayings in the other two synoptic Gospels (see Matt. 5:15; Mark 4:21). In two cases, Jesus makes a point about bringing dark acts to light: ânothing is secret, that shall not be made manifest; neither any thing hid, that shall not be knownâ (8:17; also Mark 4:22). In both cases, the sayings follow the parable of the sower and its explanation. Hence, they derive from the same source, with the real possibility that Luke is here copying from Mark. The other versions of the saying differ totally both in setting and in application. According to Matthew, after Jesus introduces the image of the lamp during the Sermon on the Mount, he then says that his followers are to let their âlight so shine before men, that they may see your good works, and glorify your Father which is in heavenâ (Matt. 5:16). In Lukeâs record, Jesus brings up lamps after discussing signs of the end-time (see 11:29â33). He next compares the light from a glowing lamp to our inner light: âThe light of the body is the eye: therefore when thine eye is single, thy whole body also is full of light; but when thine eye is evil, thy body also is full of darknessâ (11:34). Plainly, Matthew and Luke record similar sayings of Jesus who, on the spot, makes different applications of the sayings. They are not the same.74 Instead, we reckon with the likelihood that Jesus, a superb teacher, does not repeat an important teaching merely once; in their turns, the Gospel writers hand us different versions of what he says. Like all skilled teachers, Jesus repeats similar stories, with variations, and applies them to different situations that his disciples will face.75

We find a similar situation when we look at Jesusâ important Sermon on the Mount of Olives (see 21:5â36; the Note on 21:7). According to Matthew and Mark, virtually all of Jesusâ teachings in this sermon occur only on the mount and nowhere else (see Matt. 24â25; Mark 13). But Luke records words of Jesus that he speaks on two occasionsâboth before and during the sermon. First, in a preface to that sermon Jesus warns that, specifically at the temple, âthe days will come, in the which there shall not be left one stone upon another, that shall not be thrown downâ (21:6; see Matt. 24:2; Mark 13:2). Three or four days before this scene, according to Luke, as Jesus sits astride a donkey at the summit of the Mount of Olives (see 19:41), he likewise warns that âthe days shall come upon thee [the city of Jerusalem], that thine enemies . . . shall lay thee even with the ground, . . . and they shall not leave in thee one stone upon anotherâ (19:43â44). Here Jesus utters similar words, this time aiming them at the city itself.

Next, in the same sermon delivered to the Apostles atop the Mount of Olives, Jesus predicts that witnesses âshall see Jerusalem compassed with armies,â and will see its âdesolationâ following soon thereafter (21:20), a prophecy Matthew and Mark tie to âthe abomination of desolationâ that appears âwhere it ought notâ (Matt. 24:15â16; Mark 13:14). Notably, a few days before, while sitting on the same donkey on the top of the Mount of Olives, Jesus says that Jerusalemâs âenemies shall cast a trench about thee, and compass thee round, and keep thee in on every sideâ (19:43). This latter prophecy, uttered in the presence of a large multitude, not just the Apostles, is intended to send a warning to the citizens of the city and countryside, as is the later prophecy spoken to the women of Jerusalem (see 23:28â30). If they will hear, all the better for them. The prediction that Jesus shares with his Apostles in his Sermon on the Mount of Olives is aimed at men who will pay close attention to his words, with the happy result that the Christians in the city will escape the ravages of the Roman army that are heaped on Jerusalem in AD 70 (see the Notes on 19:43â44; 21:20; 23:28; the Analysis on 19:41â44).

Among the hallmarks of the Sermon on the Mount of Olives stand Jesusâ words about the unexpected arrival of the Son of Man (see 21:34â36; Matt. 24:42â44; Mark 13:32â37). But as Luke records matters, Jesus speaks about this approaching event long before he arrives in the capital city. In a series of stories that deal with readiness (see 12:35â59), he holds up in parable formââye yourselves [are] like unto men that wait for their lordâ (12:36)âan illustration about being prepared: âif the goodman of the house had known what hour the thief would come, he would have watched. . . . Be ye therefore ready also: for the Son of man cometh at an hour when ye think notâ (12:39â40). This saying is almost identical to what we find in Matthew 24:43â44, within the report of the Sermon on the Mount of Olives. Do we therefore conclude that Luke moves this saying to this earlier context in chapter 12? Not at all. First, as we have already noted, Jesus the teacher does not simply utter a principle or teaching once, expecting followers to recall what he says from that one occasion.76

A second point is even more significant. It has to do with the Joseph Smith Translation. Simply stated, the JST affirms the authenticity of this saying in its present context, even adding emphasis to the principle of readiness. For example, the JST introduces the expression, âVerily I say unto you,â just before âBlessed are those servants, whom the lord . . . shall find watching,â lending authority to what follows in verses 12:39â40 (12:37; JST 12:40). In addition, a completely revised verse is attached to 12:37 that discloses multiple arrivals of the Savior in this world, all occurring at night and thereby signaling the need for readiness: âFor behold, he cometh in the first watch of the night, and he shall also come in the second watch, and again he shall come in the third watchâ (JST 12:41). Another new saying of Jesus appears after 12:38. Within it we read words that underscore his sudden arrival: âAnd now, verily I say these things unto you, that ye may know this, that the coming of the Lord is as a thief in the nightâ (JST 12:44). This new saying is then further augmented with a warning from Jesus that, against the one who âwatcheth not his goods, the thief cometh in an hour of which he is not aware, and taketh his goods, and divideth them among his fellowsâ (JST 12:45). But Joseph Smith is not finished. He makes it clear that it is the disciples, not Jesus, who, grasping Jesusâ point, âsaid among themselves, If the goodman of the house had known what hour the thief would comeâ (12:39; JST 12:46). Plainly, all of these changes underline the authenticity of Jesusâ saying, in this place and on this occasion, about readiness (see the Analysis on 12:35â40).

F. Relationship to the Book of Acts

The same author writes both this Gospel and the book of Acts. All commentators share this conclusion, even if a few are unwilling to call the writer by the name Luke. He thus stands as the author of fully one-quarter of the New Testament. The unity of the two books manifests itself initially in the dedication of Lukeâs works to a man named Theophilus, a Roman official who is likely the patron who pays for the publication of Lukeâs volumes (see 1:3; Acts 1:1; the Note on 1:3).77 As already noted, in modern scholarship, the study of Luke and Acts together forms a standard approach.78

The relationship between the two works runs deep. Simple matters such as the same interests, or vocabulary and verbal expressionsâthese latter items are often unusual among New Testament writingsâappear regularly within the Gospel and book of Acts, confirming a single hand and mind that composes both.79 For instance, Luke brings forward Abraham as the person in whom family ties and covenant relationships and salvation all meet.80 In contrast, Mark mentions Abraham once (see Mark 12:26) and three of Matthewâs seven references occur in his opening genealogy (see Matt. 1:1, 2, 17). Another important unifying theme has to do with respect for law, a characteristic of those tied to the beginning of the Christian movement, often in contrast to fellow Jews (see 2:4â5, 22, 24, 27, 39, 42, 51; 4:16; Acts 13:45, 50; 14:19; 17:5, 13; the Notes on 1:6; 23:56). A further unifying feature consists of Lukeâs universalism, his interest in Gentiles and Jesusâ outreach to them and, later, Paulâs mission among them. As we have seen, this theme appears first in the words of Simeon who meets Jesusâ parents in the temple and, while cradling the infant in his arms, speaks of Godâs âsalvation . . . prepared before the face of all people; A light to lighten the Gentilesâ (2:31â32; see the Note 2:31). In vocabulary matters, as an example, Luke prefers the Greek term enÅpion to express the sense of âbeforeâ or âin the presence ofâ81 in contrast to the common emprosthen82 which Matthew and Mark use exclusively with the same meanings.

G. Relationship to Gospel of Mark
