The word âcomplexâ represents accurately the relationship between the Gospels of Mark and Luke. Most commentators hold that Luke copies much of Mark and a second source, âQâ (from the German word Quelle, âsourceâ), rather slavishly, introducing changesâat times, major changesâthat suit his purposes.83 In contrast, I judge that Luke draws directly from Mark only a little, almost as an afterthought, as if he already possesses a draft of his Gospel when he first sees Mark. Luke certainly takes material from Mark because the latterâs Gospel is most likely the first written and is tied to the reminiscences of Peter, thus bearing his authority.84 Indeed, Luke acknowledges that âmany have taken in hand to set forthâ accounts of Jesusâ life and ministry, implying that he draws from those reports, written and oral, which must include Mark (1:1). But for the most part, when the accounts of Mark and Luke intersect, enough differences stand out that point to Luke learning his stories often from other sources.85 A brief review will illustrate.

Lukeâs major omission of Marcan materials occurs at 9:17â18 where, at first glance, he appears to skip Mark 6:45â8:26. Within these verses, Mark rehearses the story of Jesusâ visit to Tyre and Sidon, gentile towns north of Galilee, and his healing of a Syrophenician womanâs daughter (see Mark 7:24â30). As we notice earlier, if Luke is carefully following Markâs Gospel, this omission is most puzzling, mainly because it fits neatly with his interest in narrating Jesusâ outreach to Gentiles. Similarly, in the account of the cleansing of the temple, Luke does not preserve the phrase âof all nationsâ from Jesusâ words that appears in Markâs report: âIs it not written, My house shall be called of all nations the house of prayer?â (Mark 11:17; quoting LXX Isa. 56:7). If Luke is reproducing Markâs text, the omission is glaring, knowing of Lukeâs deep interest in Gentiles (see the Notes on 2:31â32; 19:46).

But omissions are not the whole story. Far from it. Let us begin with the calling and announcing of the Twelve, a pivotal event. According to Mark, Jesus ascends âa mountainâ and chooses âtwelve, that they should be with him, and that he might send them forth to preachâ (Mark 3:13â14). Immediately after he sets them apart, he and they âwent into an houseâ where they are besieged by a âmultitude . . . so that they could not so much as eat breadâ (Mark 3:19â20). Lukeâs picture differs almost totally. It does portray Jesus ascending âa mountain,â but specifically, Jesus goes there âto pray, and [he] continued all night in prayer to Godâ (6:12). After praying about and for the Twelve through the night, and likely for others who oppose him the day before (see the Note on 6:12), he chooses the Twelve and then descends with them to âthe plainâ where he will heal afflicted persons and give a landmark sermon (see 6:13â49). The appearance of the Twelve at his side signals to his audience that they belong to him. In a word, it forms the public announcement of the organization of his church (see the Notes on 6:13, 17; the Analysis on 6:13â16; 6:17â19; 9:1â6).

In a second important case, Luke narrates Jesusâ cleansing of the temple very differently from how Mark recounts events. Both Gospel writers hold that Jesus arrives in the capital city late in the day, certainly a Sunday afternoon (see the Note on 19:5). For Mark, Jesus enters the temple and âlooked round about upon all things.â Afterward, âhe went out unto Bethany with the twelveâ (Mark 11:11). Only âon the morrowâ do âthey come to Jerusalem: and Jesus went into the temple, and began to cast out them that sold and bought in the temple,â not even permitting anyone to âcarry any vessel through the templeâ (Mark 11:12, 15â16). Thus Jesus waits until Monday to undertake his drastic action. In contrast, Luke writes that, when Jesus arrives at the city on Sunday afternoon, he âwent [directly] into the temple, and began to cast out them that sold therein, and them that boughtâ (19:45). Hence, Jesusâ actions are immediate and straightforward, the actions of a king coming to his city (see the Notes on 19:5, 45; the Analysis on 19:1â10; 19:28â40; 19:45â48).

The Words of Institution, as they are known, spoken by Jesus at the Last Supper, a key moment in his ministry, differ notably from Mark to Luke. According to Mark, Jesus breaks the bread and then says, âTake, eat: this is my body.â At the end of the meal, after lifting his own cup of wine and giving thanks and handing it to his disciples, he utters the words, âThis is my blood of the new testament, which is shed for manyâ (Mark 14:22â24). Lukeâs report, by contrast, agrees closely with the Apostle Paulâs record, the earliest account, but not with Mark. According to Luke, after breaking the bread and giving it to the disciples, Jesus declares, âThis is my body which is given for you: this do in remembrance of meâ (22:19; see the Note thereon). Paul preserves the imperatives from Mark, âTake, eat,â but then repeats exactly the words of the Savior as they appear in Luke, with one exception: âthis is my body, which is broken for you: this do in remembrance of meâ (1 Cor. 11:24; emphasis added). But here, the verb âbrokenâ (Greek participle klÅmenon), although it points to Jesusâ act of breaking the bread, does not appear in the best and earliest manuscripts of First Corinthians and is therefore suspect, bringing Paulâs report closer to that of Luke.86

Concerning the wine in the cup, Luke writes the words of Jesus thus: âThis cup is the new testament in my blood, which is shed for youâ (22:20). Clearly, Markâs report focuses on the wine (âThis is my blood,â Mark 14:24), Lukeâs on the cup (see the Note on 22:20). Even so, the two reports are rather close. Importantly, Paulâs record expands Jesusâ last expression as it appears in Luke, âwhich is shed for you,â to âthis do ye, as oft as ye drink it, in remembrance of meâ (1 Cor. 11:25). In this light, what can we conclude? First, Lukeâs account of Jesusâ words stands close to the earliest report from Paul. Second, Lukeâs rehearsal does not rest on Markâs narration. Rather, it is independent (see the Analysis on 22:14â20). I suspect that Lukeâs long association with Paul informs their mutually compatible reports.87

A final example comes from the crucial scene wherein Jesus is hailed before a hastily called council of Jewish officials after his arrest (see 22:63â71). Luke shares only a few details with Mark (see Mark 14:53, 55â65). All else is unique to his report, encouraging us to conclude that his account rests on other information. Of course, a few points of similarity exist. For instance, in 22:63 Luke writes that âthe men that held Jesus mocked him, and smote him.â Mark also paints a scene of the arresting party roughing Jesus up. Specifically, like Luke, Mark records that someone covers Jesusâ face; like Luke, Mark writes that the crowd clamors for a prophecy from Jesus (see the Notes on 22:63â64; Mark 14:65). But the differences are too many to theorize Luke borrowing from Mark. For example, according to Mark, the authorities accuse Jesus of blasphemy. In a reversal, Luke writes that those holding Jesus speak blasphemously (see the Note on 22:65; Mark 14:64). Further, Luke reports that the hearing occurs in âtheir councilâ room and that âit was dayâ (22:66) whereas Mark implies that it all takes place earlier, âin the morningâ (Mark 15:1). Moreover, Mark narrates the high priestâs dramatic ripping of his robes and his shouted charge that Jesus utters blasphemy, pieces of the story that Luke seems not to know from his source (see Mark 14:63â64).88 In addition, Mark goes on at length about the arrival and impotence of false witnesses, a set of scenes that Luke does not include (see the Note on 22:71; Mark 14:55â59). In the end, the one witness who matters is the Savior, a fact that Luke stresses. The one person in charge is Jesus (see the Analysis on 22:63â71). To reach these observations, Luke draws on a source other than Mark,89 or he willfully makes wholesale changes, a view that does not harmonize with him as an authorized âmessenger of Jesus Christâ (JST 1:1).

H. Relationship to Gospel of Matthew

As in the prior section on Markâs Gospel, the relationship between Matthewâs record and Lukeâs bristles with complexity. And it is beyond the scope of this study to explore this relationship at length. The possible connections are made complex in part because of the theorized existence of a written document called âQ,â a source that some believe lies behind stories and sayings that Luke and Matthew seem to have in common with each other but no one else. This source is thought to consist of more than two hundred verses shared between the Gospels of Matthew and Luke.90 One major problem with this view has to do with the lack of copies of such a document from antiquity. None exist (see the Analysis on 9:1â6; 9:51â56).

A large number of passages that Matthew and Luke share arises in the Sermon on the Mount (see Matt. 5â7) and the Sermon on the Plain (see 6:20â49), sermons that some believe are variants of one another.91 In spots, the similarities are intriguing. For example, at the beginning of each sermon Jesus repeats a series of beatitudes (see 6:20â23; Matt. 5:3â12). Further, one of Jesusâ main messages moves love of enemies into the foreground (see 6:27â35; Matt. 5:43â47). Moreover, when speaking of our relationships to others, he appeals to us to turn our other cheek to the one who slaps us on the face (see 6:29; Matt. 5:39â40), and then he draws us to the contrast between a speck in another personâs eye and the beam or log that is jammed into our own eye and blocks our sight (see 6:41â42; Matt. 7:3â5). Finally, he ends the sermons by appealing to the image of the wise man and foolish man who build their homes on solid and loose foundations respectively (see 6:48â49; Matt. 7:24â27). But the similarities do not take us much farther. The differences do. As I read the two texts, both the locales and the content differ substantially (see the Analysis on 6:20â49).

Concerning locations, Matthew writes that Jesus âwent up into a mountain.â When he is ready, âhis disciplesâ join him to hear his words (Matt. 5:1). This setting matches most places on the north end of the Sea of Galilee, where the hills rise abruptly only a few hundred yards north of the shoreline. Such an area does not match Lukeâs words about âthe plainâ where Jesus delivers a major sermon to a huge, diverse crowd, not just to his disciples (6:17; see JST Matt. 7:1). Instead, we think of the wide, flat region that runs along the northwest shore of the lake, extending to high cliffs to the west, now called the Valley of Ginosar. Here is the most natural setting for the Sermon on the Plain, a mere three miles west of Capernaum, after Jesus comes down from a night spent in prayer on a mountain (see the Notes on 6:12, 13, 17). In a word, the two sermons tie to different locales that lie three or so miles apart.

Concerning content, the two sermons lack basic links to each other. The beatitudes, for instance, are completely different and carry dissimilar messages. In Matthew, they set out Jesusâ fundamental expectations for his followers (see Matt. 5:3â12). In Luke, Jesusâ words offer needed comfort and promise to those who suffer reversals in this life (see the Notes on 6:20â23). According to Lukeâs record, Jesus follows the beatitudes with a series of woes against those who believe incorrectly that the prosperous circumstances of their lives are a reward of some sort (see the Notes on 6:24â26). Matthew records no such statements.

In the Sermon on the Mount, Matthew gives a lot of space to Jesusâ words on alms and prayers and fasts (see Matt. 6:1â18). Significantly, in this section of the sermon Jesus is asking us to consider why we do what we do in a setting of faith. He lifts up the ever-present question of noisy, public style versus quiet, personal worship. Nothing of this kind appears in Lukeâs record of the Sermon on the Plain. To be sure, according to Luke, Jesus repeats the Lordâs prayer and sayings about basic needs and treasures, all themes found in Matthewâs account, but in a totally different setting, an observation that suggests another source and another occasion (see 11:1â4; 12:22â34; the Analysis on 11:1â4; 12:22â34; Matt. 6:9â14, 19â21, 25â34).

In the journey section (see 9:51â19:48), Luke repeats items that Matthew also features. But in many cases, the differences point to occasions that are not the same. For example, when we turn to the parable of the great supper (see 14:16â24), we find ourselves face to face with a story retold in similar words in Matthew (see Matt. 22:1â14). For Luke, Jesus repeats the parable during his journey to Jerusalem and while reclining as a dinner guest in âthe house of one of the chief Phariseesâ (14:1). According to Matthew, he recites the story as he teaches in the temple after arriving in Jerusalem (see Matt. 21:23). For Luke, the host in the parable is âa certain [unnamed] manâ and the occasion remains unspecified (14:16). According to Matthew, the host is âa certain kingâ and the occasion is the âmarriage for his sonâ (Matt 22:2). For Luke, the invited guests simply make excuses to the one servant whom the host sends to remind them of the supper (see 14:17â20). According to Matthew, some of the invited guests treat the kingâs servantsâpluralâshamefully and kill them, bringing his wrath upon themselves (see Matt. 22:5â7). For Luke, the host fills up his home specifically with âthe poor, and the maimed, and the halt, and the blindâ (14:21; see the Notes on 14:13, 21). According to Matthew, the king fills the banquet room in the palace with âboth bad and good . . . guestsâ (Matt. 22:10). For Luke, one of Jesusâ conclusions is that the kingdom is open to âthe poor, the maimed, the lame, the blindâ (14:13; see the Notes on 14:13â14). Another point is that ânone of those men which were bidden shall taste of my supperâ (14:24), that is, the Jewish authorities who believe that they will join in the messianic banquet will not be allowed (see the Note on 14:15). According to Matthew, Jesusâ point is that âmany are called, but few are chosenâ (Matt. 22:14; see the Analysis on 14:15â24). In this light, it is a natural step to conclude that Luke âdraws from his independent source.â92

In some instances, the teachings of Jesus mirror one another as we move from Matthew to Luke, except the setting, opening again the possibility that Jesus repeats basically the same words on different occasions. As an example, both Gospel writers preserve Jesusâ astute statement on the impossibility of serving two masters (see 16:13; Matt. 6:24). In Matthew, it appears in Jesusâ Sermon on the Mount and largely stands apart from the sayings that Jesus brings up before and after it. In Luke, Jesus repeats his declaration at the end of his parable of the unjust steward where it applies to the stewardâs situation of divided loyalties and to the challenges that his disciples will face when making choices in the world (see 16:14â18; the Analysis on 16:13â18).

A second example consists of Jesusâ parable of the lost sheep (see 15:1â7; Matt. 18:12â14). In Matthew, the parable sits amidst a series of teachings against offending children. In fact, at the end of the parable, he quotes Jesus as concluding, âit is not the will of your Father which is in heaven, that one of these little ones should perishâ (Matt. 18:14). According to Luke, Jesus rehearses the parable when pressed by âthe Pharisees and scribesâ about his association with âsinnersâ (15:1â2; see the Notes and Analysis thereon). Matthew quotes Jesus as saying, âif so be that he [the owner] find it [the sheep]â (Matt. 18:13), whereas Jesus says in Lukeâs version that the owner searches âuntil he find itâ (15:4). Matthew records that, after the owner of the sheep finds the lost one, âhe rejoiceth more of that sheep, than of the ninety and nineâ (Matt. 14:13). In contrast, Luke reports that the owner invites âfriends and neighboursâ to a joyous celebration over the sheepâs recovery and that âin heavenâ joy will erupt âover one sinner that repenteth, more than over ninety and nine just personsâ (15:6â7). Thus, Jesusâ parable in Luke focuses on the recovery of lost souls, rather than on children. Not surprisingly, this theme continues through the following parables of the lost coin (see 15:8â10) and the prodigal son (see 15:11â32). It therefore seems evident that Jesus repeats the two stories on different occasions to different audiences in order to make a different point (see the Analysis on 15:3â7).93

I. Independence from Other Gospels

From our review in the last two sections above, it becomes clear that connections exist between Lukeâs Gospel and those of Mark and Matthew. Certainly Mark, and possibly Matthew, are among the âmanyâ accounts that preserve Jesusâ life and ministry which Luke knows about and draws from (1:1). But each story, each event must be examined to determine whether we can conclude that Luke adopts it or whether he learns it from another source. It is customary among commentators to lump all of Lukeâs unique stories into a single source called âL,â indicating Lukeâs special information. But because we do not know whether some items come from a written document or whether he learns others from oral interviews, we cannot hold that âLâ is a single, unified source.94 We just know that, by his diligent efforts, he brings together as much as he is able into a unified âdeclarationâ or narrative (1:1; Greek diÄgÄsis; see the Analysis on 1:1â4).95 Let us examine a few passages that point to compelling evidence for Lukeâs independence from Mark and Matthew.

Perhaps oddly, Luke seems to share intriguing connections with the Gospel of John, mostly manifested in seemingly small details. The ties are close enough that one scholar judges that âLuke and John were relying on two allied streams of oral tradition.â96 Possibly, possibly not. But a taste of common elements will bear out some sort of link. For instance, both list a second man named Judas among the Twelve (see 6:16; John 14:22); both hold that Satan enters Judas and inspires the betrayal (see 22:3 and the Note thereon; John 13:27); both write that the high priestâs servant loses his right ear in the scuffle at the arrest (see 22:50; John 18:10); both record that Pilate declares Jesus to be innocent no fewer than three times (see 23:4, 14, 22; John 18:38; 19:4, 6); and both note that two angels appear to announce Jesusâ resurrection (see 24:4; John 20:12).97 None of these details appear in Matthew and Mark.

One of the most memorable of Jesusâ sayings deals with âfaith as a grain of mustard seedâ (17:6; see also Matt. 17:20). The saying catches our attention both because of the tiny size of this seedâit is among the smallest of seedsâand because of the principle that faith usually starts from small beginnings (see the Note on 17:6).98 But we notice first that Matthew and Luke feature the saying in different places in Jesusâ story. According to Matthew, Jesus teaches the principle of faith after some of his disciples botch the healing of a young boy while Jesus is on the Mount of Transfiguration (see Matt. 17:14â16). For Luke, Jesus utters the saying after âthe apostlesâ beg of him, âIncrease our faithâ (17:5). According to Matthew, Jesus stresses the enormous power of faith by promising, âye shall say unto this mountain, Remove hence to yonder place; and it shall remove; and nothing shall be impossible unto youâ (Matt. 17:20; also Matt. 21:21; Mark 11:22â23; see the Note on 3:5). In Luke, the power promised by Jesus bursts forth in the following way: âye might say unto this sycamine tree, Be thou plucked up by the root, and be thou planted in the sea; and it should obey youâ (17:6; see the Note thereon). The two imagesâmoving a mountain and moving a tree by exercising faithâare not the same, leading Claus-Hunno Hunzinger to judge that Lukeâs version is the original saying of Jesus.99 But as we see in prior examples, our brief review leads us back to an earlier conclusion: that Luke preserves one version of Jesusâ saying that he utters on two occasions to different audiences to make separate points (see the Analysis on 17:5â10).

Lukeâs account of Jesusâ experience in Gethsemane differs notably from the reports of Matthew and Mark. Luke does not repeat the name Gethsemane, as Matthew and Mark do (see Matt. 26:36; Mark 14:32), but he calls it âthe place,â signaling it as not only the spot for Jesusâ suffering but also its special, sacred character (see the Note on 22:40). Luke seems to know of only one visit by Jesus to his three chief Apostles during his night of anguish (see the Notes on 22:45â46) rather than the three narrated in the other Gospels (see Matt. 26:40, 43, 45; Mark 14:37, 40, 41). Further, in a disputed passage, Luke reports the visit of an angel, âstrengthening [Jesus]â (see the Note on 22:43). The other accounts preserve no such narrative. Moreover, only Luke writes of Jesus sweating âgreat drops of blood falling down to the groundâ (22:44). None of the other Gospels preserve this most important scene although other scripture does (see Mosiah 3:7; D&C 19:17â18) as do the early Christian authors Justin Martyr, Irenaeus and Tatian (see the Note on 22:43). These major differences between Luke and the other Gospel writers nod toward Luke being independent from the others as he carefully reconstructs the scene in Gethsemane (see the Analysis on 22:39â46).

We turn to a final illustration. Luke alone records words of Jesus as he walks to his crucifixion. Only Luke shows us that the mortal Savior does not stop his efforts to reach out to people after his trial before Pilate. Instead, Jesus desperately tries to warn them of the calamities that will descend on Jerusalem. Twisting in discomfort toward the âgreat company of people, and of womenâ who are following him, he shouts out particularly to the women, âDaughters of Jerusalem, weep not for me, but weep for yourselves, and for your children.â Why? Because âthe days are coming, in the which they shall say, Blessed are the barren, and the wombs that never bare.â The situation in the city will become so awful that inhabitants will âbegin to say to the [surrounding] mountains, Fall on us; and to the [nearby] hills, Cover usâ (23:27â30). As gloomy additions of the Joseph Smith Translation to the Gospels of Matthew and Mark affirm, âgreat tribulations [shall descend] . . . upon the inhabitants of Jerusalem; such as was not before sent upon Israel, of God, since the beginning of their kingdomâ (JST Matt. 24:18; JST Mark 13:20). Thus Jesus, in an overflowing compassion that ignores his own pains, extends himself again in warning to those whose children will suffer. All this comes to us from Luke (see the Analysis on 23:27â33).

J. The New Rendition

This book cites the King James Version because of its standing as the preeminent English text. Alongside it in this book is a new rendition by Eric D. Huntsman. My thanks go to Eric for allowing me to include it in this volume. Huntsmanâs version carries a more literal and modern sense of the Greek text. The elegance of the latter translation does not rise to the level of the KJVâno English translation does100âbut it serves as a guide to readers when passages seem difficult to grasp. More than that, taking the two translations together allows us to gain a fuller sense of what Luke intends his audience to understand. The new rendition grows mainly out of the Nestle-Aland critical edition of the Greek text in Novum Testamentum Graece, including down to and especially the most recent edition, the twenty-eighth (2012).101

One passage especially in Lukeâs Gospel has come under scrutiny because this passage is found chiefly in later copies of the text of Luke and is missing from the earliest manuscript (P75) and other important manuscripts. Of Jesusâ suffering in Gethsemane, the KJV reads, âAnd there appeared an angel unto him from heaven, strengthening him. And being in an agony he prayed more earnestly: and his sweat was as it were great drops of blood falling down to the groundâ (22:43â44). Even though the Nestle-Aland text and a majority of modern translations indicate that this passage is likely inserted later into Lukeâs account, other scholars accept its originality because it is unlikely to have been invented.102 As noted earlier, one reason for taking it seriously is because early Christian authors Justin Martyr, Irenaeus, and likely Tatian, all from the second century, know of this passage that attests Jesus bleeding while in Gethsemane. A second reason for being open to its originality rests on modern scripture. One passage prophesies about Jesusâ bleeding: âfor behold, blood cometh from every pore, so great shall be his anguish for the wickedness and the abominations of his peopleâ (Mosiah 3:7). In a second, the Risen Savior himself refers back to this moment: â[this] suffering caused myself, even God, the greatest of all, to tremble because of pain, and to bleed at every pore, and to suffer both body and spiritâ (D&C 19:18). Hence, for Latter-day Saints the observation that Jesus suffers terribly in Gethsemane and bleeds from his pores is secure (see JST 22:43â44; the Notes on 22:43â44).

IV. Distinctive Teachings

A. Property and Money

Few topics blossom as fully in Lukeâs Gospel as do property and money. It appears that, by preserving a multitude of Jesusâ teachings on property and money, Luke is sharing with us his own fresh set of priorities, priorities shaped after his conversion to his new faith. As his two booksâLuke and Actsâdemonstrate, he is an educated man. To acquire that education, and then to devote it to researching and writing as he does, requires leisure and money. We can readily judge that, early in his life, these things are important to him, perhaps of supreme importance. But after he becomes converted to the Risen Christ, the priorities in this life change. His selection of stories and sayings apparently mirrors that change. From many examples,103 we can examine only a few.

We readily behold that property is a major issue because its lackâpovertyâstands so stark and prominent at the beginning and ending of Jesusâ life. The stories about his motherâs poor offering after his birth (see 2:24) and the poor widowâs offering in the temple (see 21:1â4) form an inclusio that bridges from the first to the last of Lukeâs record, underlying its unity. By including these accounts, Luke adroitly angles our minds toward the issues of poverty and wealth (see the Analysis on 2:21â24; 21:1â4).

In Jesusâ first teaching on poverty and wealth, framed in his Sermon on the Plain, he intones, âBlessed be ye poor: for yours is the kingdom of God. Blessed are ye that hunger now: for ye shall be filledâ (6:20â21; see the Notes thereon). Following these sweeping promises, he then issues warnings: âwoe unto you that are rich! . . . Woe unto you that are full!â Why such warnings? Because âye [wealthy people] have received your consolationâ (6:24â25). We sense immediately that such people receive all that they will enjoy in this life with nothing to enjoy in the next life, unless they turn to the Savior and undergo a change of heart about their wealth, as we shall soon see (see the Notes on 6:24; 18:27).104

A number of Jesusâ parables concern money and property. One of the most prominent and longest is the parable of the prodigal son (see 15:11â32). This parable brims with property matters, including the division of the fatherâs estate, the depletion of younger sonâs portion through âriotous living,â the hiring of this son by âa citizen of that country . . . to feed swine,â the older sonâs rightful claim to âserveâ his father faithfully as he honorably prepares himself to inherit the estate, and the fatherâs assurance to his older son, âall that I have is thineâ (see 15:12â13, 15, 29, 31; the Notes on 15:12, 31). Although the main thrust of the parable concerns the recovery of the lost, that is, the lost younger son, a secondary push has to do with an inheritance that the younger son can or will no longer possess.

In one of his most direct declarations on money, Jesus talks about taxes (see 20:20â26). In a word, we owe them to the state. During the last week of his life, some of those assigned to watch him, so âthat they might take hold of his words [and] . . . might deliver him unto the power and authority of the governor,â approach him with a burning question: âIs it lawful for us to give tribute unto Caesar, or no?â (20:20â22). The question grows out of a growing resistance movement whose members come to call themselves âZealotsâ and choose to stand against the Roman government, which includes not paying taxes. Eventually, those who hold this view, because of their militaristic rebellion, will help to bring down the might of Rome on their people in the Jewish war of AD 66â70, a conflict that Jesus predicts will bring on his people unparalleled death and destruction (see the Note on 20:25; the Analysis on 20:20â26).

Perhaps the most notable event in Jesusâ ministry where he deals with wealth concerns his meeting with the rich ruler and, especially, his following comments (see 18:18â30). Both Matthew, who alone calls this man âyoung,â and Mark preserve this account (see Matt. 19:16â30; Mark 10:17â31). Jesusâ words to the fellow, of course, drive home a startling point about the manâs wealth and its massive weight against him inheriting âeternal lifeâ (18:18): âsell all that thou hast, and distribute unto the poor, and thou shalt have treasure in heaven: and come, follow me.â Stunned, the ruler becomes âvery sorrowfulâ and turns away because âhe was very richâ (18:22â24). Hence, the manâs wealth squarely blocks his path to eternal life. Jesus illustrates in dramatic fashion the impossibility of this fellowâs situation: âit is easier for a camel to go through a needleâs eye, than for a rich man to enter into the kingdom of Godâ (18:25; see the Note thereon). Jesusâ followers are just as surprised and, standing bewildered, ask: âWho then can be saved?â Jesusâ response seemingly softens the blow of his earlier words: âThe things which are impossible with men are possible with Godâ (18:26â27).

But what exactly does Jesus mean? The Prophet Joseph Smith senses the ambiguity and adds words of Jesus unrecorded by Luke, and lacking in Mark and Matthew as well. These words uncover the meaning of Jesusâ declaration. The first part fits with what Jesus says elsewhere, but the second part changes the landscape for the wealthy: âIt is impossible for them who trust in riches, to enter into the kingdom of God; but he who forsaketh the things which are of this world, it is possible with God, that he should enter inâ (JST 18:27; emphasis added; see also JST Matt. 19:26; JST Mark 10:26). This single statement flings the door wide open to the wealthy to âinherit eternal lifeâ (18:18; see the Notes on 18:24â25, 27; the Analysis on 16:1â12; 18:26â30).

B. The Church

The question whether the Savior organizes a church during his mortal ministry must first deal with his attitude toward the institutions of worship in his day, the synagogue and temple. His attitude is glowingly positive. To be sure, people tied closely to synagogue and temple launch themselves against him, trying to discredit him and, ultimately, to kill him (see 13:14; 19:47; 20:1; 22:2, 52; 23:10, 23). Even so, he respectfully turns these institutions into places of teaching and miracle working. In fact, Luke features the youthful Jesus accompanying his parents to the temple and therefore mirroring their practice (see 2:41â51), and later observes that âhis custom was [to go] . . . into the synagogue on the sabbath dayâ (4:16). Near the end of his life, he calls the temple âMy houseâ and âthe house of prayerâ (19:46; see the Note thereon). In this light, we conclude that he holds the synagogue and temple in very high regard and models a behavior for his followers to emulate (see 21:37; 24:53; Acts 2:46; 5:12).105

Perhaps surprisingly, Jesusâ own visible valuation of the synagogue begins with the temptations from the devil (see 4:1â13). In short, the temptations bore into the question about power and authority: Who possesses the real power and authority? The devil? Jesus? It seems odd that, on the spot, Jesus does not respond with force to the devilâs challenges. âIf thou be the Son of Godâ (4:3, 9); âIf thou . . . wilt worship meâ (4:7). Instead, he quietly quotes scripture and leaves the questions unanswered. âIt is writtenâ (4:4, 8); âIt is saidâ (4:12). But does he softly sidestep them? No. After the temptations, Luke follows Jesus back to Galilee and ultimately to Nazareth, his hometown, where we find him in âthe synagogue on the sabbath dayâ (4:16). Friends and family members and acquaintances, hearing of his earlier activities in Capernaum (see 4:23), wait with bated breath as he stands to read from scripture: âThe Spirit of the Lord is upon me,â he recites, âbecause he hath anointed me to preach the gospel to the poor; he hath sent me to heal the brokenheartedâ (4:18). In these words stands the declaration of his authorityâin the heart of a synagogue service (see the Note on 4:18; the Analysis on 4:16â30). But there is more.

The next Sabbath we find Jesus down in Capernaum, more than twenty miles away, inside the synagogue of course (see 4:31, 33). Remarkably, inside the building, âthere was a man, which had a spirit of an unclean devilâ (4:33; see the Note thereon). To bring relief to the man, Jesus rebukes the unclean demon, driving him out of the man (see 4:34â35). We can predict the reaction of the congregation members: âthey were all amazed.â Then Luke quotes the response of some in attendance: âwith authority and power he commandeth the unclean spirits, and they come outâ (4:36; emphasis added). Here, on the lips of attendees is the affirmation of Jesusâ authority and power after they witness a manifestation of his authority and power inside the synagogue. We emphasize, as Luke does, that both Jesusâ miracle and the congregationâs immediate response take place in a synagogue service, on the Sabbath day. What can we therefore conclude? It becomes clear that Jesus not only reverences the synagogue and the Sabbath day but also consciously chooses the synagogue in Nazareth to announce his authority and the synagogue in Capernaum to show his power. The synagogueâa place of worship, of prayer, of scripture reading, of devotion, of holy ordinances, of sacred learningâis the place where Jesus responds to the devilâs challenge about power and authority (see the Note on 4:36). Because within days he will call the first four men who will serve as Apostles (see 5:1â11), it is evident that the synagogue, an institution of worship, forms the launchpad for his own church organization (see the Analysis on 4:1â13).

Within Lukeâs Gospel we begin to see much of the outline of his church organization. Naturally, Jesus first calls the Twelve. And he guides the process. He first calls key men to follow him, initially the two pairs of fishermen brothers, Peter and Andrew, James and John, and then Levi, the customs agent (see 5:1â11, 27â28; the Notes on 5:6â7). After reaching out to these brethren, he allows the natural process of personal attraction to him and his message to swell the ranks of followers so that, a few weeks later, he can go âinto a mountain to prayâ about choosing âtwelveâ men from their number âwhom also he named apostlesâ (6:12â13). That same morning, after calling these twelve men, he leads them down to âthe plainâ where they meet the larger number âof his disciplesâ as well as âa great multitude of people,â some of whom come from far away âto hear him, and to be healed of their diseasesâ (6:17). The scene becomes the formal announcement of the Twelve, the anchors of the churchâs organization (see the Notes on 6:17; 9:1â2; the Analysis on 5:1â11; 6:17â19; 9:1â6).106 But Jesus does not stop here. He regularly offers instruction to disciples, more specifically to the Twelve, throughout his ministry (see 12:36â38; 16:8; 22:25â27; the Notes on 8:51, 54; 9:50, 60; 10:2; 12:39, 41â43, 48; the Analysis on 9:49â50; 12:41â48).

We turn next to women. Conceptually, Jesus features them as full members of his kingdom in a simple pair of parables. In the first, he compares the kingdom to âa grain of mustard seed, which a man took, and cast into his garden,â underscoring work typically done by a man. He then compares the kingdom to âleaven, which a woman took and hid in three measures of meal,â highlighting an act usually performed by a woman (13:18â21). In these two brief illustrations, Jesus turns his social and legal world upside-down. For in the Ancient Near East, in any period, women stand as second-class citizens. No so for Jesusâ kingdom. They will enjoy full membership on an equal plane with men (see the Note on 13:21; the Analysis on 13:18â21).

On a practical level, following Lukeâs first notice of âthe twelveâ accompanying Jesus on a preaching tour, he writes about âcertain womenâ from Galilee who receive ministrations from Jesus and also minister âunto him of their substanceâ (8:1â3). Such a place of honor, next to the Twelve, signals their high importance among Jesusâ closest followers. They will reappear at the cross and at the tomb, following him to Jerusalem and thereby becoming first-rank witnesses of his resurrection (see 23:49, 55â56; 24:1â11, 22â24).107 In addition, though not noted, they most certainly assist in the preparations for and the serving of the Last Supper (see the Note on 22:8). Their involvement in these significant events, and their close tie to the Twelve, underscore their roles as primary participants in Jesusâ emerging church (see Acts 1:14).108 From what we can learn about them, they appear very much like an ancient Relief Society (see the Notes on 8:1â3; 23:56; the Analysis on 22:7â13; 23:50â56).

Another highly visible group consists of the Seventy (see 10:1â12, 17â20). Jesus calls these men from the growing entourage of followers and sends them on a preaching mission. To them he gives instructions similar to the Twelve (see 9:1â6); to them he gives priesthood power to âheal the sickâ (10:9); for them he suspends food laws, indicating that their mission will reach out to Gentiles (see the Notes on 10:7â8). Thus Jesus creates a second quorum of leaders in his church, this one with a charge to reach beyond fellow Jews to embrace Gentiles (see the Notes on 8:38â39; 10:1; the Analysis on 8:26â40; 10:1â12).109 Modern scripture affirms this very assessment: âThe Seventy are also called to preach the gospel, and to be especial witnesses unto the Gentiles and in all the worldâthus differing from other officers in the church in the duties of their callingâ (D&C 107:25).

Little noticed are individuals who function essentially as heralds for Jesus, much as John does, although John prepares for Jesus generally and these people form an advance team that travels to specific destinations.110 As we have already seen, when Jesus turns deliberately toward Jerusalem and away from Galilee, he âsent messengers before his face.â Their task is not to preach but âto make ready for himâ (9:52). They are to announce Jesusâ coming in towns and villages before he arrives, thus assuring that citizens have opportunity to see and hear the Son of God and alerting townspeople that a large group will be arriving so that they have goods at hand to supply them with food and places to lodge. These messengers are an obvious key to the success of Jesusâ preaching mission. In the modern church, such people are Aaronic Priesthood holders, who are to assist church leaders âto make appointments, and to prepare the way . . . this is the way that mine apostles, in ancient days, built up my churchâ (D&C 84:107â8), duplicating the pattern established here by Jesus (see the Notes on 9:49â50, 52; the Analysis on 9:49â50).

In light of what we introduce above, we conclude that long before Jesus arrives in Jerusalem for the last time, he organizes his church and establishes important patterns of missionary work and church service. He authorizes men and women and perhaps even youths. His intent is to spread his message to as many people as possible and to give them opportunity to accept that message.

C. Keys

Only Matthew documents a known statement from the mortal Jesus about keys: âI will give unto thee [Peter] the keys of the kingdom of heaven,â he intones, âand whatsoever thou shalt bind on earth shall be bound in heaven: and whatsoever thou shalt loose on earth shall be loosed in heavenâ (Matt. 16:19). Plainly, these keys open the powers of sealing and unsealing earthly matters, rendering them effective or ineffective in the next life. In Luke, keys lie just out of sight, but are present nonetheless. He quotes Jesusâ summarizing of a famine condition in the days of Elijah: âmany widows were in Israel in the days of Elias, when the heaven was shut up three years and six monthsâ (4:25). Clearly implicit are keys that lock and unlock the heavens, blocking or allowing passage of blessings from one sphere to another (see Rev. 11:6).111

Near the end of his Gospel, Luke writes of the experience of the two disciples who walk with and then refresh themselves with the Resurrected Christ in Emmaus. At a certain moment, âtheir eyes were opened, and they knew [Jesus]â (24:31). For a second time, keys are assumed, this time to open the eyes. In the very next verse, the two disciples say âone to another, Did not our heart burn within us, . . . while he opened to us the scriptures?â (24:32). As is now obvious, keys are required to open our understanding of the scriptures. In fact, this same use of keys plays out later that evening in the presence of a number of disciples: âThen opened he their understanding, that they might understand the scripturesâ (24:45). These keys, designed to lock and unlock the heavens and to lock and unlock peopleâs eyes and the scriptures, differ in function from those promised to Peter. But they are just as real and point to the variety of keys that the Savior turns in our behalf (see 11:52; 13:25; Rev. 1:18; 3:7; 9:1; 11:6; 20:1; the Notes on 24:31â32, 45).112

Lukeâs narrative thus presents a series of passages that have to do with keys, specifically from the mortal viewpoint, that is, how they affect us, whether opening our eyes or opening our ability to comprehend. Modern scripture offers a similar picture, but from the divine perspective: âthe light [of Christ] which shineth, which giveth you light . . . is the same light that quickeneth your understandingsâ (D&C 88:11; see the Notes on 24:31â32). The Savior possesses the keys to open the door to proper understanding, namely, to understand as God does.113

D. Delay of Second Coming

Almost all commentators agree that statements about the delay of the Saviorâs Second Coming arise from early Christians themselves who, expecting him to return immediately, have to deal with his nonappearance. But indicators exist that Jesus is the author of this delay, an observation that rests firmly in other scripture. The earliest signal appears in a vision of Enoch wherein, after seeing the resurrection, the seer asks the Lord, âWilt thou not come again upon the earth?â What is the response? âAs I live,â declares the Lord in an oath, âeven so will I come in the last days, in the days of wickedness and vengeanceâ (Moses 7:59â60). Thus, long before Jesus comes upon the earth as an infant, he promises that he will return again, but only âin the last days.â In this connection, those who are converted in the age of the Apostles come to Christ because he will âmanifest [himself] unto them . . . by the Holy Ghost,â not by personal appearance (3 Ne. 15:23). The Apostle Paul spells out the delay in different terms. To the Thessalonian saints he pleads that they not be âshaken in mind, or be troubled, neither by spirit, nor by word, nor by letter as [if] from us, as that the day of Christ is at hand.â That day will only arrive after âthere come a falling away first,â that is, an apostasy (2 Thes. 2:2â3).

In Lukeâs record, we encounter Jesusâ first attempt to give contour to this teaching when, at a distance from Jerusalem, he laments, âO Jerusalem, Jerusalem, which killest the prophets, . . . how often would I have gathered thy children together, . . . and ye would not! . . . Verily I say unto you, Ye shall not see me, until the time come when ye shall say, Blessed is he that cometh in the name of the Lordâ (13:34â35). The Joseph Smith Translation adds a strip of authenticity to this lament by noting Jesusâ action in an insertion before this saying: âin this very hour he began to weep over Jerusalem.â The JST then buttresses the saying by adjusting and adding words of Jesus in its midst: âverily I say unto you, Ye shall not know me, until ye have received from the hand of the Lord a just recompense for all your sinsâ (JST 13:34, 36; emphasis added; see the Notes on 13:34â35; the Analysis on 13:31â35). The sense is that, after the inhabitants suffer, the Savior mercifully will allow them to come to know him as he is (see Isa. 40:1â2).

No more than a few weeks later, Jesus gives voice again to his Second Coming. On this occasion, he addresses specifically his disciples: âThe days will come, when ye shall desire to see one of the days of the Son of man, and ye shall not see it.â Why not? Because the Son of Manâs coming will be later. He follows these words almost immediately with a further hint at delay: âas the lightning, . . . so shall also the Son of man be in his day,â coming suddenly and brilliantly (17:22, 24). The Joseph Smith Translation adds weight to the distant approach of the Second Coming by changing this last verse to read: âas the light of the morning, . . . so shall also the Son of man be in his dayâ (JST 17:24; emphasis added). The brilliance remains, but the suddenness diminishes (see the Notes on 17:22â24; the Analysis on 17:20â37). In sum, it is just as reasonable to see Jesus offering such teaching to his disciples as they approach the capital city as it is to try to make this instruction a part of the drama-filled days in Jerusalem.114 In light of the JST additions and adjustments, it is clear that Jesus announces the delay, not others.

As evidence that Jesus and his followers discuss his Second Coming before arriving in the city we cite his words repeated to them as they gather together atop the Mount of Olives for his important sermon there: âAs ye have asked of me concerning the signs of my [second] coming, in the day when I shall come in my glory in the clouds of heavenâ (D&C 45:16). Here is the tipoff, in Jesusâ own words, that on earlier occasions he and they converse about his Second Coming. But what about the delay? Jesus goes on: âconcerning Jerusalem . . . when that day shall come, shall a remnant [of Jerusalemâs residents] be scattered among all nations; But they shall be gathered again; but they shall remain until the times of the Gentiles be fulfilledâ (D&C 45:24â25). Clearly, Jesus envisions a long delay that involves a scattering and gathering of his people as well as blessings coming to the Gentiles. Concerning that era of the Gentiles, âhe answered them, and said, In the generation in which the times of the Gentiles shall be fulfilled, there shall be signsâ (JST 21:25). Notably, the signs that Jesus discloses in his sermon on the Mount of Olives will appear chiefly in that distant age (see the Notes on 21:25â28; the Analysis on 21:25â28).

With the help of modern scripture, therefore, we arrive at a clear understanding that Jesus himself teaches about a delay of his Second Coming. To be sure, some early Christians mistakenly believe that his reappearance is near. But that expectation of an immediate return is incorrect, as Paul reminds church members living in Thessalonica (see 2 Thes. 2:2â3).

E. Satan

Satan is a real personality, not a figment of peopleâs imagination. And, as Luke shows in his narrative, Satan possesses genuine powers, including his own kingdom (see the Note on 11:18). For example, he has the ability to diminish, even take, divine light that a person receives through Godâs word; he can degrade a personâs well-being as he does to the woman afflicted for eighteen years; he possesses power to break a personâs commitment to the Savior as he seeks to do with Peter; more frightening still, he can directly influence a person to do his will, if the person becomes a willing host, as Judas does. Notably, modern scripture affirms this brief photograph of the devil (see the Notes on 8:12; 13:16; 22:3, 31; the Analysis on 8:26â40; also Alma 5:41; 34:35; 40:13; 3 Ne. 18:18; D&C 10:10, 15, 20â21; 29:40; 78:10). But there is more.

Satanâs minions come in multiples and can behave in the same way that he does. After all, he captures a third of the spirits in the premortal struggle for dominance and they are now under his influence (see Moses 4:6; D&C 29:36â37; Rev. 12:4). For instance, seven come to inhabit Mary Magdalene and, in a merciful moment, are cast out; a large number, that call themselves âLegion,â take possession of the gentile man on the east side of the Sea of Galilee and, as in Maryâs situation, are driven out by Jesusâ power (see the Notes on 8:2, 27, 29â33). Hence, the devil possesses an army of willing demons that do his bidding (see the Notes on 11:17â18).

But Satan and his demon forces are subject to the Saviorâs power. Again and again in Gospel stories, Jesus forces them from their temporary roosts in accord with a prophecy uttered more than a century before his birth: â[the Savior] shall cast out devils [in his mortal ministry], or the evil spirits which dwell in the hearts of the children of menâ (Mosiah 3:6). Jesus first signals that he can both resist Satanâs temptations and also overcome them when the two of them tussle in the desert (see 4:1â13). Within days, Jesus follows his victory in the wilderness by summarily tossing out one of Satanâs minions from a beleaguered man in the Capernaum synagogue (see 4:33â35). Plainly, Jesus is taking back territory over which Satan exerts power (see the Analysis on 10:17â20). Further, as we have noticed, Jesus asserts his own power over the devil when he portrays âa strong manâ in âhis palaceâ whose âgoods are in peaceâ until assailed by âa stronger than heâ who overcomes âhim, [and] . . . taketh away from him all his armour wherein he trustedâ (11:21â22). This scene pictures Satan, the âstrong man,â resting at ease in someoneâs home, now designated âhis palace,â until âa stronger than he,â the Savior, comes along and pushes him out of that home. The contest is over control of the home wherein Jesus challenges Satanâs grip. It is significant that Jesus invokes images of the home to affirm his power over the devil and his minions (see the Notes on 11:21â22; the Analysis on 11:14â28).

V. Character of Lukeâs Gospel

A. Date and Place of Composition

The question of dating Lukeâs Gospel always ties to the Jewish revolt of AD 66â70. Do hints exist that he is writing after the war? Yes and no. On the yes side, stand passages that stress the law-abiding character of Christians, often in contrast to their Jewish neighbors (see 1:6; 2:4â5, 22, 24, 27, 39, 42, 51; 20:46â47; etc.; Acts 13:50; 14:2, 19; 17:5, 13; etc.). This dimension is evidently a natural outcome of the war wherein most inhabitants of the Roman world do not know how to distinguish Christians and Jews and may well assume that all Christians are sympathizers and fellow citizens with the Jewish rebels. Luke apparently senses the need to make a distinction for his reading public (see the Analysis on 2:21â24). Luke constructs the second leg of the yes response when he writes that, before him, âmany have taken in hand to set forth . . . a declarationâ of Jesusâ story (1:1). If Markâs Gospel is among those many, and it likely is, then Luke writes his Gospel following the war, judging that Mark composes his Gospel in the late sixties of the first century (see section III.G above).115 The third part of the yes answer arises from Jesusâ predictions of the terrible fate of the city during the war, predictions seemingly too detailed to look forward to Jerusalemâs fall but, rather, grow out of hindsight (see 13:35; 19:41â44; 21:6, 20â24; 23:28â31).116 But this latter point rests squarely on the view that no one can predict exact details in the future, a notion that modern studies and modern scripture undercut (see D&C 45:18â25).117 To be sure, Luke likely composes his Gospel after the fall of the city, but he is a faithful reporter of the Saviorâs predictions (see the Analysis on 19:41â44; 21:5â6, 20â24).

On the no side, we find no clear cases of Lukeâs account looking back to the fall of Jerusalem. First, the usual attempt to seize on Jesusâ language of 21:20 (âye shall see Jerusalem compassed with armiesâ) falls flat because we know that Jesus can predict the future, even with high precision. Second, this event becomes a major moment in the ancient world and we find no allusions to the cityâs collapse in Lukeâs book of Acts where he might foreshadow the punishment of Jewish officials and individuals through the cityâs destruction, punishment for unleashing persecution against the Saviorâs followers (see Acts 4:1â3; 5:17â18, 26â28; 6:9â13; 7:54â59; 8:1â3; etc.). Third, Luke shows no knowledge of the Apostle Paulâs fate in his book of Acts, how his hearing before the emperor goes (see Acts 25:9â12; 28:16), whether Paul travels to Spain as he plans (see Rom. 15:24, 28), or whether he is executed following Neroâs burning of Rome in AD 64, as hinted by Clement of Rome, who writes about AD 96 (see 1 Clem. 5:3â7).118

In this light, it is impossible to fix a date, even an approximate date, for Luke writing his two-volume work, the Gospel and the book of Acts. On balance, it seems that a date between AD 70 and 80 settles most easily within the evidence, all of which is circumstantial.

The place where Luke composes his Gospel remains unknown. Early and modern attempts locate Lukeâs efforts in Rome, Achaia, Boeotia, Antioch, or Caesarea; all rely on little or no evidence.119 Most authors will agree that Luke writes his Gospel outside of ancient Palestine. But in light of his grasp of geography, even this judgment is subject to question (see section V.C below).

B. Luke the Man

Luke, âa messenger of Jesus Christâ (JST 1:1), is obviously an educated man skilled in composing in his native language, Greek. Both of his books exhibit a finely attuned ability to communicate well, and they form a genuine history.120 Further, he stands as a second or third generation believer. How so? Because he writes of others, not himself, as âeyewitnesses, and ministers of the wordâ (1:2). Plainly, he looks backward in time to the life and ministry of his Lord. Early Christian tradition puts his residence in Antioch, far to the north of ancient Palestine, in the rather late, so-called âAnti-Marcionite Prologueâ to Lukeâs Gospel. But this information is open to question.121 This said, can we know anything more about him?

The Apostle Paul offers a substantial hint in his epistle to the Colossians about who Luke is: âLuke, the beloved physician, and Demas, greet youâ (Col. 4:14; see also 2 Tim. 4:11; Philem. 1:24). From every appearance, including especially the âweâ passages in Acts, Luke accompanies Paul for extended periods of time (see Acts 16:10â17; 20:5â21:18; 27:1â28:16). Hence, it is natural to see him with Paul when he writes the Colossians letter while a captive in Rome122 or, possibly, in Ephesus123 (see Col. 4:3, 10, 18; also 1:24). Indeed, we know that Luke spends time with Paul while he is a prisoner in Rome (see Acts 28:16); and he may well enjoy a previous acquaintance with âthe elders of the churchâ in Ephesus who come to Miletus at Paulâs request (Acts 20:16â18). Hence, Paul possesses opportunity to mention Luke as if he is known to church members in Colossae.

But do these observations hold up to scrutiny? The primary protest against Lukeâs companionship with Paul arises from the observation that the theology of Paulâs speeches recorded in Acts do not accord with the theological viewpoints in his epistles. But the evidence is mixed. First of all, Luke does not set out to chronicle Paulâs correspondence, or to summarize it. Second, Paulâs impromptu speeches will surely differ from his carefully thought-out letters. Third, the corpus of Paulâs letters will not be gathered until about AD 90, perhaps a decade or more after Luke pens his Gospel and book of Acts. Such a collection, of course, will influence how anyone writes about Paulâs activities. But Luke does not fall under such an influence.124

The more focused question has to do with whether Lukeâs Gospel shows evidence of a physicianâs touch. At the outset, we take notice of H. J. Cadburyâs study which demonstrates that apparent instances of medical vocabulary in the Gospel mirror the language in ancient, nonmedical works.125 Hence, vocabulary by itself, such as the term for leprosy (Greek lepra), does not show Luke to be a physician (see 5:12â13; the Notes thereon). When it comes to possible interest in medical matters, some passages show Lukeâs elevated concern and other passages do not. We offer four examples of each.

Standing as evidence for Lukeâs physician background are the following. First, when Jesus heals Simon Peterâs mother-in-law of a fever, Luke characterizes the illness as âa great feverâ (4:38), a detail that a physician will notice but is missing from Markâs story (see the Notes on 4:35, 38; Mark 1:30â31). Second, in narrating Jesusâ restoration of the withered hand of the man in a synagogue, Luke writes that it is his âright hand [that] was witheredâ (6:6), an observation that a physician will mark, whereas Mark preserves no such remark (see Mark 3:1). Third, Luke notes that the âright earâ of the high priestâs servant is cut off at the time of Jesusâ arrest (see 22:50; also John 18:10) whereas Mark writes no such detail (see Mark 14:47). Fourth, Luke alone notes that, on the spot, Jesusâ heals the ear of this servant (see 22:51; the Notes on 22:50â51). In sum, Luke plainly makes observations that we expect a physician to make.126 But is this the whole story?

On the side that Luke is not a physician, we note the following instances. First, when writing about Jesus healing Peterâs mother-in-law, Luke omits Markâs expression about Jesus taking her by âthe hand,â a seemingly odd omission for a physician (4:39; see the Note thereon; Mark 1:31). Second, in contrast to Markâs detail that the woman with âan issue of bloodâ âfelt in her body that she was healed of that plague,â Luke leaves out her sense that her whole body is healed after touching Jesusâ garment (see 8:44â45; Mark 5:25, 29). Third, according to Luke, Jesus asks a father to lead his afflicted boy to him for a cure (Greek verb prosagÅ), implying his ability to walk (see 9:41), whereas according to Mark the boy is carried (Greek verb pherÅ), a curious adjustment given a physicianâs interest in the condition of a patient (Mark 9:20).127 Fourth, Matthew records that, when Jesus heals the blind men at Jericho (Matthew pictures two blind men), Jesus âhad compassion on them, and touched their eyes,â details that a physician will surely pay attention to but do not appear in Lukeâs account (see 18:42; Matt. 20:34).

Naturally, the strength of the negative observations depends in large part on the relationship of Lukeâs Gospel especially to Markâs Gospel and, to a lesser degree, to Matthewâs report. As we see in our earlier review, the connections are not necessarily robust (see sections III.G and III.H). Nevertheless, Lukeâs interest in medical matters, if he is a physician, does not push itself prominently into view in his Gospel narrative.

C. Geography and Topography

Above, we lay out the case for Luke spending time in Jerusalem when he accompanies the Apostle Paul to the city, remaining at least two weeks and perhaps much longer. Hence, he enjoys an opportunity to see the topography of the area as well as the relationship of parts of the city to their surroundings (see section II.E). That is certainly the case with Bethphage, which lies on the route to Jerusalem from Jericho (see 19:28â29). He also knows that a person can enter the temple directly from the east and north without going through the city (see 19:45).128

The more pressing and interesting question is whether Luke knows the geography of Galilee in the north.129 Even though he evidently spends much or all of two years with Paul in Caesarea, a seaside city (see Acts 27:1â2),130 we do not know whether he travels into the area of Jesusâ youth and early ministry. Most scholars simply dismiss Lukeâs understanding of Galilean topographical features, claiming that his account of events in the north is vague at best. But is this the whole story? Do we find elements that point to Lukeâs firmer grasp of the area? A few examples turn our gaze in a different direction.

First of all, Luke knows of the long descent from Nazareth to Capernaum (see 4:31). From some source, perhaps his own knowledge, he understands that the Sea of Galilee and its shoreline towns lie well below the hilltop village of Nazareth (see the Note on 4:31). In this connection, moreover, Luke knows the direction of the wind that rakes across the lake during a stormâit rushes âdownâ to the surface from the surrounding heights (8:23). Matthew and Mark preserve no such notation (see Matt. 8:24; Mark 4:37). So they are not Lukeâs source (see the Note on 8:23). Furthermore, in the account of Jesusâ trip to the east side of the lake where he heals a demoniac, Luke adds the expression âover against Galileeâ to clarify the locale of miracle (8:26). Again, neither Matthew nor Mark carry a notation with this kind of specificity (see the Note on 8:26; Matt. 8:28; Mark 5:1). In addition, Luke consciously situates the stories of Jesusâ long journey to Jerusalem into a region generally south of Galilee (see 9:51â19:40), rimming them along the imprecise border with Samaria (see 9:52â53; 17:11; see the Notes on 9:52 and 17:11; the Analysis on 9:51â56; 17:11â19).

Above all, indicators of Lukeâs basic grasp of Galilean topography rise within the setting for Jesusâ Sermon on the Plain. Throughout the night before the sermon, Jesus prays on âa mountain.â In the morning, from his followers he selects the âtwelveâ whom he names âapostles,â and brings them with him to âthe plainâ where he will speak to âa great multitudeâ (6:12â13, 17). For those who hold that Jesusâ Sermon on the Plain and Sermon on the Mount are variant versions of the same sermon, the notation about âthe plainâ creates a problem because no such place exists along the northern shore of the Sea of Galilee, the evident place where Jesus delivers his Sermon on the Mount. For here the ground slopes up from the shoreline for a short distance before rising steeply into the surrounding heights. But a completely different topography presents itself on the northwest corner of the lake, a mere three miles from Capernaum (see the Notes on 6:12, 17). There, setting off a rather level place, mountains and cliffs surround an extended plain that reaches inland and westward about a mile from the edge of the lake. This spot matches exactly Lukeâs topographic description of land forms for Jesusâ calling of the Twelve and for his sermon (see the Analysis on 6:20â49). Furthermore, this plain is the natural place for hearers from âTyre and Sidonâ to arrive from the north and west because the road for them comes down through the Arbel Pass onto this level area (see the Note on 6:17).131 In fact, if Luke indeed visits Nazareth and Capernaum, he travels this same road.

Reasons for seeing Luke unacquainted with Galilean geography include the following. First, he omits Capernaum as the place for Jesusâ healing of the paralyzed man who is carried on a stretcher (5:17; see Mark 2:1; also Matt. 9:1). If Luke knows Markâs account, the omission is curious unless he has some other purpose in mind, such as universalizing Jesusâ action by separating it from its geographical context (see the Note on 5:17). Second, he leaves out the location Caesarea Philippi for Peterâs confession of Jesus as âThe Christ of God,â a geographical remark preserved in the other synoptic Gospels (see 9:18â20; Matt. 16:13; Mark 8:27). In this case, the city of Caesarea Philippi lies far to the north of the Sea of Galilee and, if Luke visits the Galilee area, he very possibly does not travel far from the lake. After all, nothing distinctive from Jesusâ ministry occurs between Caesarea Philippi and the environs of the Sea of Galilee. Third, Luke neglects pointing to Jesusâ ministry âbeyond Jordan,â that is, on the east bank of the river (Matt. 19:1; Mark 10:1). Instead, he inserts rather vague notices of Jesusâ travel with disciples after departing Galilee (see the Notes on 9:51; 10:38; 14:25; 17:11; 18:35; 19:28). But much of this travel runs along and through the Jordan Valley. If Luke, in fact, comes to Galilee from Caesarea on the Mediterranean coast, he will surely return the same way rather than roaming through the Jordan Valley, taking a very roundabout and climatologically hot route back to the Mediterranean Sea which will have schooled him in the difficulty of the ascent to Jerusalem from Jericho, a climb that he does not seem to appreciate (see the Note on 19:28). In sum, we conclude that none of these omissions rule out the distinct possibility that Luke possesses a basic acquaintance of Galilean geography from his own visit.

One final observation is in order. Although all the Gospels preserve the miracle of the feeding of the five thousand, Luke is the only writer to identify its locale: âa desert [uninhabited] place belonging to the city called Bethsaidaâ (9:10). How does he know this fact? The most natural response is that he learns it from someone who earlier is present, either from one of the early disciples or from one residing in the area. Because Bethsaida sits near the north shore of the Sea of Galilee a mere three miles east of Capernaum, a place easily reached by boat or by foot (see Matt. 14:13; Mark 6:32â33; John 6:17), and because from other clues noted above Luke likely visits this shoreline region, he is evidently in a firm position to point to the location of this remarkable event (see the Note on 9:10).
